The focus on intrinsic hallucinations in the context of complex financial tabular data is a relatively underexplored area, addressing a critical gap in existing evaluation methodologies. The taxonomy of financial reasoning types (Direct Lookup, Comparative Calculation, Bivariate Calculation, and Multivariate Calculation) provides a structured and insightful framework for analyzing hallucination patterns based on task complexity.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its direct relevance to the practical deployment of LLMs in finance. The financial sector is highly regulated and demands accuracy and reliability, making hallucination a major barrier to adoption. By providing a robust evaluation framework and a finance-specific dataset, the paper offers valuable tools for researchers and practitioners to assess and mitigate the risk of hallucinations. The detailed analysis of hallucination patterns and their correlation with reasoning complexity provides actionable guidance for improving LLM performance in financial tasks. The study also correctly identifies the inadequacy of generic benchmarks, advocating for domain-specific evaluations, a crucial perspective for advancing LLM applications in specialized fields.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and clear contributions:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of hallucinations in financial LLMs and provides well-defined contributions to address this problem.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The methodology is robust and well-justified, with careful consideration of potential biases and limitations. The masking criteria (Uniqueness, Consistency, Answerability) and the precision-relaxed evaluation protocol enhance the reliability of the evaluation.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation of various LLMs on the proposed dataset, providing valuable insights into their performance and limitations. The analysis of hallucination patterns based on reasoning complexity is particularly insightful.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for in-house LLM evaluation and serves as a crucial step toward building more trustworthy and reliable financial AI systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of financial documents:** While the focus on S&P 500 annual reports is a good starting point, the framework could be extended to other types of financial documents, such as analyst reports, news articles, and regulatory filings, to enhance its generalizability.
    *   **Dependence on LLMs for answerability annotation:** While the pilot study validates the reliability of LLM-based answerability annotation, it is still a potential source of bias. A hybrid approach that combines LLM annotation with human validation could further improve the accuracy of the dataset.
    *   **Limited analysis of mitigation strategies:** The paper focuses primarily on evaluation and does not explore specific strategies for mitigating hallucinations in financial LLMs. Future work could investigate techniques such as retrieval augmentation, fine-tuning, and prompt engineering to address this challenge.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development and deployment of LLMs in finance by:
    *   Providing a standardized evaluation framework for assessing hallucination risk.
    *   Facilitating the development of more robust and reliable LLMs for financial applications.
    *   Raising awareness of the unique challenges and requirements of the financial domain.
    *   Encouraging further research on hallucination mitigation strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the field by addressing a critical problem in a high-stakes domain and by providing a novel and rigorous evaluation framework. The novelty of the automated dataset creation paradigm, finance-specific dataset, and analysis of hallucination patterns based on reasoning complexity justifies a high score. While there are some limitations in terms of the scope of financial documents and the reliance on LLMs for annotation, these are outweighed by the overall strengths of the paper. The study will likely have a positive impact on the development and deployment of LLMs in finance and serves as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners. The limitations prevent a higher score, however.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SPEX: A Vision-Language Model for Land Cover Extraction on Spectral Remote Sensing Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05202v1)
- **Authors**: Dongchen Si, Di Wang, Erzhong Gao, Xiaolei Qin, Liu Zhao, Jing Zhang, Minqiang Xu, Jianbo Zhan, Jianshe Wang, Lin Liu, Bo Du, Liangpei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Spectral information has long been recognized as a critical cue in remote sensing observations. Although numerous vision-language models have been developed for pixel-level interpretation, spectral information remains underutilized, resulting in suboptimal performance, particularly in multispectral scenarios. To address this limitation, we construct a vision-language instruction-following dataset named SPIE, which encodes spectral priors of land-cover objects into textual attributes recognizable by large language models (LLMs), based on classical spectral index computations. Leveraging this dataset, we propose SPEX, a multimodal LLM designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction. To this end, we introduce several carefully designed components and training strategies, including multiscale feature aggregation, token context condensation, and multispectral visual pre-training, to achieve precise and flexible pixel-level interpretation. To the best of our knowledge, SPEX is the first multimodal vision-language model dedicated to land cover extraction in spectral remote sensing imagery. Extensive experiments on five public multispectral datasets demonstrate that SPEX consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in extracting typical land cover categories such as vegetation, buildings, and water bodies. Moreover, SPEX is capable of generating textual explanations for its predictions, thereby enhancing interpretability and user-friendliness. Code will be released at: https://github.com/MiliLab/SPEX.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SPEX: A Vision-Language Model for Land Cover Extraction on Spectral Remote Sensing Images":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPEX, a novel vision-language model (VLM) specifically designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction from spectral remote sensing imagery.  The key innovation is the integration of spectral information, a critical but often underutilized aspect of remote sensing data, into a large language model (LLM). To facilitate this, the authors created a new dataset, SPIE (Spectral Prompt Instruction Extraction), which converts spectral characteristics of land cover objects (derived from spectral indices like NDVI, NDWI, and NDBI) into textual attributes interpretable by the LLM. SPEX utilizes a vision encoder, a multiscale feature aggregation module (MSAM), the LLM, a token compression projector (TCP), and a mask generator for pixel-level segmentation.  The model is trained in a two-stage process, including visual pre-training and visual-language training on the SPIE dataset.  Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate SPEX's superior performance compared to existing methods and its ability to generate textual explanations for its predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a novel approach by directly incorporating spectral information into a vision-language model for land cover extraction. Existing VLMs in remote sensing often overlook spectral characteristics, making SPEX a unique contribution. The creation of the SPIE dataset is another important aspect.  Converting spectral information into a language format using spectral indexes helps existing LLMs better understand what they are looking at. This method is much better than just simply relying on pretrained datasets, because it allows for better interpretability and controlability. The MSAM and TCP modules seem well-designed to enhance feature extraction and reduce computational overhead.

**Significance:**

SPEX shows strong potential for practical applications in land cover mapping and monitoring. Its ability to understand instructions and generate textual explanations enhances user interaction and trust in the model's predictions. The superior performance compared to existing methods on multiple datasets validates its effectiveness. However, the paper's reliance on existing spectral indices is a potential limitation. While effective, these indices may not capture all the nuances of spectral information, and exploring more advanced spectral feature engineering techniques could further improve performance. Also, the choice of datasets may limit generalizability.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The idea of integrating spectral priors into a vision-language framework for land cover extraction is innovative.
*   **High-Quality Dataset:** The SPIE dataset seems well-constructed and addresses a key limitation in existing remote sensing VLM research.
*   **Strong Performance:** Extensive experiments demonstrate SPEX's superiority over existing methods.
*   **Interpretability:** The ability to generate textual explanations is a significant advantage for practical applications.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the model architecture, training process, and experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Spectral Indices:** Using basic spectral indices might limit the model's ability to capture all available spectral information.
*   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** Although multiple datasets are used, their overall characteristics might restrict the model's generalization capability to truly diverse geographical regions and land cover types.
*   **Computational Cost:** Training large VLMs can be computationally expensive, and the paper doesn't fully address the practical implications for real-world deployment.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While component ablation is performed, a more thorough analysis of design choices within the MSAM and TCP modules could strengthen the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of remote sensing by demonstrating the value of incorporating spectral information into VLMs.  It can inspire further research into more sophisticated spectral feature engineering techniques and the development of more robust and interpretable remote sensing models. The SPIE dataset can serve as a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

SPEX represents a significant step forward in applying vision-language models to remote sensing by explicitly addressing the importance of spectral information. The creation of the SPIE dataset and the strong experimental results are particularly noteworthy. While the paper has some limitations, including its reliance on basic spectral indices and the computational cost associated with large language models, the novelty and significance of the approach warrant a strong score. The paper has a clear potential to influence future research and development in the field. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, especially regarding how well existing spectral features are incorporated into a language processing model. The main limitations are the use of existing spectral indexes. It may not completely capture all available data and would require specialized training. Also, the datasets could have some biases that limit the overall generalizablity of the data.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReasoningTrack: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05221v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Wang, Liye Jin, Xufeng Lou, Shiao Wang, Lan Chen, Bo Jiang, Zhipeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Vision-language tracking has received increasing attention in recent years, as textual information can effectively address the inflexibility and inaccuracy associated with specifying the target object to be tracked. Existing works either directly fuse the fixed language with vision features or simply modify using attention, however, their performance is still limited. Recently, some researchers have explored using text generation to adapt to the variations in the target during tracking, however, these works fail to provide insights into the model's reasoning process and do not fully leverage the advantages of large models, which further limits their overall performance. To address the aforementioned issues, this paper proposes a novel reasoning-based vision-language tracking framework, named ReasoningTrack, based on a pre-trained vision-language model Qwen2.5-VL. Both SFT (Supervised Fine-Tuning) and reinforcement learning GRPO are used for the optimization of reasoning and language generation. We embed the updated language descriptions and feed them into a unified tracking backbone network together with vision features. Then, we adopt a tracking head to predict the specific location of the target object. In addition, we propose a large-scale long-term vision-language tracking benchmark dataset, termed TNLLT, which contains 200 video sequences. 20 baseline visual trackers are re-trained and evaluated on this dataset, which builds a solid foundation for the vision-language visual tracking task. Extensive experiments on multiple vision-language tracking benchmark datasets fully validated the effectiveness of our proposed reasoning-based natural language generation strategy. The source code of this paper will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/Open_VLTrack
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning Track: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of long-term vision-language tracking (VLT), where existing methods struggle with variations in the target object's appearance over extended video sequences. The authors propose "ReasoningTrack," a novel framework that uses a pre-trained vision-language model (Qwen2.5-VL) to dynamically update the natural language description of the target object during tracking. The key idea is to leverage the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to adapt the text descriptions as the tracking progresses.  The model is optimized using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with GRPO.  The updated language description is then combined with visual features in a unified tracking backbone.  The paper also introduces a new large-scale, long-term VLT dataset, TNLLT, containing 200 video sequences. The effectiveness of ReasoningTrack is demonstrated through extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks, including TNLLT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core novelty lies in using LLM chain-of-thought reasoning for *dynamic text adaptation* in the context of VLT.  While dynamic text adaptation is not entirely new in VLT, the use of LLMs with explicit reasoning is a significant step forward.  Previous methods have updated text descriptions through attention mechanisms or direct text generation without providing clear justification for those updates, lacking interpretability.
    *   The introduction of the TNLLT dataset is another significant contribution, addressing the lack of large-scale datasets for long-term VLT. The meticulous annotation with object attributes and a reasoning chain will provide an excellent resource for the community.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's approach has the potential to improve the robustness and accuracy of VLT, especially for long-term scenarios where the appearance of the target object changes significantly. The dynamic adaptation of text descriptions provides more flexibility than methods that rely on a static initial description.
    *   The interpretability offered by the chain-of-thought reasoning aspect is also valuable, as it makes it easier to understand and debug the tracking process, and build trust in results, especially in critical applications.
    *   The TNLLT dataset is a timely and valuable contribution, providing a new benchmark for evaluating VLT algorithms under realistic and challenging conditions.  Retraining and evaluating 20 visual trackers establishes a strong baseline for comparison.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing VLT methods and articulates the need for dynamic text adaptation.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** The ReasoningTrack framework is well-designed and integrates the LLM reasoning module seamlessly with a unified tracking backbone.
    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on multiple benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of ReasoningTrack compared to existing methods.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The TNLLT dataset is a valuable resource for the VLT community, enabling future research on long-term tracking and reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The integration of large pre-trained models introduces a high computational cost, limiting the tracking speed.  This needs to be addressed for real-time applications. The paper acknowledges this limitation but does not offer specific solutions.
    *   **Fixed Update Interval:** The fixed update interval for text descriptions is a potential limitation.  Dynamically adjusting the update interval based on the confidence of the tracker or the magnitude of appearance changes could further improve performance.
    *   **Tracker Failure:** The paper acknowledges a failure case where the tracker could not maintain the correct target, even with a reasonable textual description, implying dependence on image context, and not text alone, which can become a limiting factor. This issue is not fully resolved or investigated.
    *  **Over-reliance on visual elements in system prompts:** The system prompts in the LLM focus on analyzing "visual elements", potentially creating a dependency on visual cues and hindering the ability to track objects with significant appearance change.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the VLT field by:

    *   Promoting the use of LLMs for dynamic text adaptation in VLT.
    *   Providing a new benchmark dataset for long-term VLT.
    *   Encouraging research on more efficient and interpretable VLT algorithms.

*   **Score Justification:**

    Considering the above, the paper is a solid contribution to the VLT field. The introduction of ReasoningTrack and the TNLLT dataset are both significant and novel contributions. Although there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. It is also well-written and clearly presents the approach and results. It will undoubtedly encourage future research in this direction.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Cross-LoRA: A Data-Free LoRA Transfer Framework across Heterogeneous LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05232v1)
- **Authors**: Feifan Xia, Mingyang Liao, Yuyang Fang, Defang Li, Yantong Xie, Weikang Li, Yang Li, Deguo Xia, Jizhou Huang
- **Abstract**: Traditional parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA are tightly coupled with the base model architecture, which constrains their applicability across heterogeneous pretrained large language models (LLMs). To address this limitation, we introduce Cross-LoRA, a data-free framework for transferring LoRA modules between diverse base models without requiring additional training data. Cross-LoRA consists of two key components: (a) LoRA-Align, which performs subspace alignment between source and target base models through rank-truncated singular value decomposition (SVD) and Frobenius-optimal linear transformation, ensuring compatibility under dimension mismatch; and (b) LoRA-Shift, which applies the aligned subspaces to project source LoRA weight updates into the target model parameter space. Both components are data-free, training-free, and enable lightweight adaptation on a commodity GPU in 20 minutes. Experiments on ARCs, OBOA and HellaSwag show that Cross-LoRA achieves relative gains of up to 5.26% over base models. Across other commonsense reasoning benchmarks, Cross-LoRA maintains performance comparable to that of directly trained LoRA adapters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Cross-LoRA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cross-LoRA, a novel framework for transferring LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) modules between heterogeneous Large Language Models (LLMs) without requiring any training data or further fine-tuning. Cross-LoRA uses two key components: LoRA-Align, which aligns subspaces between source and target models using rank-truncated Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) and a Frobenius-optimal linear transformation, and LoRA-Shift, which projects the source LoRA weight updates into the target model's parameter space. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of Cross-LoRA on several benchmark datasets, showing competitive performance compared to directly trained LoRA adapters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in enabling **zero-shot transfer** of LoRA adapters between different LLM architectures *without requiring any training data or fine-tuning*. This is a significant departure from prior work in parameter-efficient transfer learning, which often necessitates synthetic data generation, lightweight fine-tuning, or specially designed adapters. While techniques like knowledge distillation exist, Cross-LoRA offers a more direct and efficient transfer approach for pre-trained adapters. The use of rank-truncated SVD for subspace alignment is well-motivated and facilitates practical implementation. The Frobenius-optimal transformation, while perhaps not entirely novel in itself, is applied effectively within the context of LoRA transfer.

*   **Significance:** The ability to transfer LoRA adapters without retraining addresses a critical challenge in deploying LLMs.  LoRA's dependency on the base model architecture has been a limiting factor, especially as models evolve rapidly. Cross-LoRA has several implications:

    *   **Reduced computational cost and storage:** It eliminates the need for repeated fine-tuning, saving significant computational resources.
    *   **Knowledge sharing:** It enables the sharing and reuse of adapters across different model families, promoting collaboration and efficiency.
    *   **Model agility:** It makes it easier to switch between base models without losing the accumulated knowledge in LoRA adapters.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data-free and Training-free:**  The biggest strength is the absence of any training data or fine-tuning requirement, which simplifies and accelerates the transfer process.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experimental results are compelling, showing performance comparable to or even slightly better than directly trained LoRA adapters on some benchmarks. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of the subspace alignment mechanism.
    *   **Architecture-Agnostic:** While the results suggest architectural similarity improves transfer, the framework does work across different model families (Qwen, LLaMA, Gemma).
    *   **Efficient Implementation:** It can be deployed on commodity GPUs with limited memory, making it practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Well-motivated Methodology:** The use of truncated SVD for identifying meaningful subspaces and Frobenius-optimal transformation is theoretically sound and practical.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Performance Gap on Some Tasks:**  The paper acknowledges a slight performance gap in reasoning-heavy tasks (like HellaSwag), suggesting that some task-specific information may be lost during the projection.  Further research is needed to address this issue.
    *   **Sensitivity to Architecture Mismatches:** While the method works across different architectures, the performance is better when the source and target models have similar architectures. The dependence on the similarity of certain architectural components (GQA, SwiGLU, etc.) could limit the effectiveness in extremely dissimilar architectures.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the empirical results are strong, the paper lacks a more rigorous theoretical analysis of the error bounds for the projection and the impact of architectural differences on the transferability of knowledge.
    *   **One-shot Transfer:** The framework only considers one-shot transfer, without any post-transfer adaptation. While this is a key feature, exploring potential lightweight adaptation schemes after the transfer could further improve performance.
    *   The performance gains while impressive can be interpreted as marginal compared to baseline models in some cases.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   **Adoption in LLM Development:** Cross-LoRA could become a standard technique for managing and transferring LoRA adapters, especially in environments where data access is restricted or computational resources are limited.
    *   **Facilitation of Model Agility:** It provides a way to migrate models without retraining adapters.
    *   **New Research Directions:** It opens up new research directions in architecture-agnostic transfer learning and knowledge sharing in LLMs.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.5**.

**Rationale:** Cross-LoRA makes a significant contribution by enabling zero-shot transfer of LoRA adapters across heterogeneous LLMs, which is a major step forward in parameter-efficient transfer learning. The paper is well-written, the experimental results are convincing, and the method is practical and efficient. However, there are also limitations, including the sensitivity to architectural mismatches, performance gap in certain tasks, and limited theoretical analysis. Therefore, while the approach is novel and significant, some limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification via Chain-of-Thought Enhancement and Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05234v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan Shangguan, Xiaocui Yang, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang, Ge Yu
- **Abstract**: The surge in rich multimodal content on social media platforms has greatly advanced Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA), with Large Language Models (LLMs) further accelerating progress in this field. Current approaches primarily leverage the knowledge and reasoning capabilities of parameter-heavy (Multimodal) LLMs for sentiment classification, overlooking autonomous multimodal sentiment reasoning generation in resource-constrained environments. Therefore, we focus on the Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification task, JMSRC, which simultaneously performs multimodal sentiment reasoning chain generation and sentiment classification only with a lightweight model. We propose a Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Distillation model, MulCoT-RD, designed for JMSRC that employs a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation paradigm to address deployment constraints in resource-limited environments. We first leverage a high-performance Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) to generate the initial reasoning dataset and train a medium-sized assistant model with a multi-task learning mechanism. A lightweight student model is jointly trained to perform efficient multimodal sentiment reasoning generation and classification. Extensive experiments on four datasets demonstrate that MulCoT-RD with only 3B parameters achieves strong performance on JMSRC, while exhibiting robust generalization and enhanced interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of joint multimodal sentiment reasoning and classification (JMSRC) in resource-constrained environments.  The authors propose a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation framework, MulCoT-RD, to train lightweight Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to perform sentiment reasoning and classification without relying on computationally expensive, closed-source models. They employ a two-stage approach: (1) a Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Enhancement Module generates reasoning chains using a powerful teacher model (e.g., GPT-4o or Qwen2.5-VL-72B), and (2) a Reasoning Distillation Module transfers this knowledge to a lightweight student model (Qwen2.5-VL-3B) via a medium-sized assistant (Qwen2.5-VL-7B) using multi-task learning. Experiments on several MSA datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The novelty lies in combining structured CoT enhancement with a Teacher-Assistant-Student distillation specifically tailored for resource-limited JMSRC.  While CoT and Knowledge Distillation are established techniques, their application in this particular hierarchical manner to enable lightweight MLLMs for joint reasoning and classification is a relatively new approach.
    *   The structured CoT prompting with task decomposition, reasoning guidance, conflict mediation, and adaptive retry contributes to the quality of reasoning.
    *   The multi-task learning on the assistant model to jointly enhance sentiment label prediction accuracy and reasoning quality and using the assistant model to augment the training set adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The work addresses an important practical challenge: deploying MSA models in resource-constrained environments.  This is significant because it broadens the applicability of MSA technology to scenarios where cloud-based solutions or large models are not feasible.
    *   The results demonstrate that a 3B parameter model can achieve strong performance, rivaling or exceeding larger models.  This is a valuable contribution, showing that complex reasoning can be effectively distilled into smaller models.
    *   The method improves model interpretability by generating explicit reasoning chains, offering insights into the model's decision-making process. This is beneficial for debugging, trust, and understanding of model behavior.
    *   The rigorous experiments and comparisons across different MSA datasets and with several baselines support the claims and validate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined problem and motivation.
    *   Well-designed and explained methodology.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation across diverse datasets.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to understand the contribution of each component.
    *   Case studies that highlight the advantages of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper compares against SOTA models, a more in-depth analysis into the reasoning quality and correctness *compared to human reasoning* could strengthen the claims of enhanced interpretability. Reasoning evaluation using just embedding similarity and text metrics is insufficient to establish *human-like* reasoning.
    *   The model selection process, while justified, remains somewhat limited (just one family of models - Qwen). Investigating the generalizability with a wider range of models could further enhance the robustness of the findings. The teacher model's impact on the student's capability needs more exploration.
    *   While the concept of resource-constrained is well-defined, more granular information regarding inference time and memory usage would be appreciated, specifically to highlight the trade-off achieved by the student model.
    *   The paper touches on the lack of diversity, but it does not cover how to make it more robust to biased training examples.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The MulCoT-RD framework can serve as a benchmark and a template for future research on resource-efficient JMSRC.
    *   The proposed distillation approach can inspire similar knowledge transfer techniques for other multimodal tasks.
    *   The emphasis on interpretability can influence the development of more transparent and trustworthy MSA systems.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and practical approach to JMSRC, addressing a key limitation in the field.  The hierarchical distillation technique, coupled with structured CoT prompting, is well-designed and achieves strong results. However, the analysis of the reasoning quality and the exploration of different teacher-assistant-student model combinations and reasoning bias mitigation could be more extensive to further bolster the claims and overall impact of the work. The paper makes a solid contribution and will likely influence future research directions on resource-efficient and interpretable multimodal sentiment analysis. A stronger exploration of limitations would further increase the paper's quality.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05236v1)
- **Authors**: Yatong Lan, Jingfeng Chen, Yiru Wang, Lei He
- **Abstract**: Arbitrary viewpoint image generation holds significant potential for autonomous driving, yet remains a challenging task due to the lack of ground-truth data for extrapolated views, which hampers the training of high-fidelity generative models. In this work, we propose Arbiviewgen, a novel diffusion-based framework for the generation of controllable camera images from arbitrary points of view. To address the absence of ground-truth data in unseen views, we introduce two key components: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS employs a hierarchical matching strategy that first establishes coarse geometric correspondences using camera poses, then performs fine-grained alignment through improved feature matching algorithms, and identifies high-confidence matching regions via clustering analysis. Building upon this, CVC-SSL adopts a self-supervised training paradigm where the model reconstructs the original camera views from the synthesized stitched images using a diffusion model, enforcing cross-view consistency without requiring supervision from extrapolated data. Our framework requires only multi-camera images and their associated poses for training, eliminating the need for additional sensors or depth maps. To our knowledge, Arbiviewgen is the first method capable of controllable arbitrary view camera image generation in multiple vehicle configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models" introduces a novel framework for generating controllable camera images from arbitrary viewpoints for autonomous driving, addressing the challenge of limited ground-truth data in extrapolated views. The approach, called ArbiViewGen, consists of two main modules: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS stitches images from multiple cameras by geometric transformations and feature matching to create pseudo-ground truth data. CVC-SSL trains a diffusion model to reconstruct original camera views from the stitched images, enforcing cross-view consistency in a self-supervised manner, eliminating the need for extrapolated ground truth. The framework is designed to work with multi-camera setups common in autonomous vehicles.  A novel evaluation metric is presented based on projecting colored LiDAR point clouds onto the novel views.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Critical Problem:** The lack of ground truth data for training novel view synthesis models in autonomous driving is a significant bottleneck. ArbiViewGen directly targets this issue.
    *   **Novel Self-Supervised Approach:** The CVC-SSL strategy is a key contribution. By reconstructing original views from stitched extrapolated views, the method avoids the need for ground truth supervision at novel viewpoints. This is a very smart approach.
    *   **Purely Visual Approach:** ArbiViewGen relies primarily on multi-camera images and pose information, making it practical in scenarios where other sensor data (e.g., depth maps) may be unavailable or unreliable.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The proposed LiDAR-based evaluation strategy provides a much-needed way to quantitatively assess the quality of generated images in extrapolated views, where standard metrics cannot be easily applied.
    *   **Competitive Results:** The experimental results demonstrate that ArbiViewGen outperforms a baseline (DriveSuprim) across several quantitative metrics and qualitatively produces more realistic and geometrically consistent novel views.
    *   **Comprehensive Design:** The hierarchical approach to image stitching (FAVS) and the integration of cross-view attention in the diffusion model show a thoughtful and well-engineered design.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Camera Poses:** The method's performance is heavily reliant on the accuracy of camera pose information. Errors in pose estimation could negatively impact the quality of view synthesis. The paper does not explicitly address the robustness to pose errors.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** Diffusion models are known to be computationally intensive, and the image stitching process adds further complexity. The paper lacks a discussion of the computational cost of training and inference. More efficient architectures could be explored.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is mainly based on the nuScenes dataset. Generalization to other datasets and driving scenarios (e.g., different weather conditions, lighting) needs to be evaluated further. The novelty of the used dataset evaluation methodology doesn't seem to outweigh the generalisability concerns.
    *   **Lack of comparisons to other methods:** ArbiViewGen is evaluated against "DriveSuprim". This approach appears to be chosen solely for being rotation-based augmentation, yet it doesn't account for the wealth of research into generative models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The self-supervised learning approach for arbitrary viewpoint image generation is a significant contribution to the autonomous driving field.
    *   The framework allows for data reuse across different vehicle types, reducing the need for extensive data collection and annotation for each new vehicle model.
    *   The LiDAR-based evaluation strategy contributes a valuable tool for evaluating novel view synthesis methods in the absence of ground truth data.
    *   While the individual components (diffusion models, image stitching) are not entirely novel, the combination and application to this specific problem, along with the self-supervised training paradigm, offer significant novelty.

**Justification of Score:**

ArbiViewGen presents a novel and effective solution to a critical problem in autonomous driving. The self-supervised learning approach, the multi-camera image stitching algorithm, and the LiDAR-based evaluation strategy contribute significant value. However, the reliance on accurate camera poses, the lack of computation cost analysis, and limited evaluation data keep the score from being higher. The reliance on nuScenes also introduces a potential bias, and the comparison against DriveSuprim does not showcase the full potential against other generative methods. The architecture also doesn't introduce enough novelty, as it uses standard diffusion models and lacks comparisons to the research dedicated to multi-view generative models.

### Driver Assistant: Persuading Drivers to Adjust Secondary Tasks Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05238v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Xiang, Muchen Li, Jie Yan, Manling Zheng, Hanfei Zhu, Mengyun Jiang, Lingyun Sun
- **Abstract**: Level 3 automated driving systems allows drivers to engage in secondary tasks while diminishing their perception of risk. In the event of an emergency necessitating driver intervention, the system will alert the driver with a limited window for reaction and imposing a substantial cognitive burden. To address this challenge, this study employs a Large Language Model (LLM) to assist drivers in maintaining an appropriate attention on road conditions through a "humanized" persuasive advice. Our tool leverages the road conditions encountered by Level 3 systems as triggers, proactively steering driver behavior via both visual and auditory routes. Empirical study indicates that our tool is effective in sustaining driver attention with reduced cognitive load and coordinating secondary tasks with takeover behavior. Our work provides insights into the potential of using LLMs to support drivers during multi-task automated driving.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper.

**Summary**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to create a "driving assistant" that proactively persuades drivers in Level 3 automated vehicles to maintain attention to road conditions and reduce engagement in distracting secondary tasks.  The LLM-based tool leverages road conditions and driver behavior (identified through eye-tracking) as triggers for persuasive interventions. These interventions are designed to be "humanized" and provide gentle, informative advice instead of abrupt warnings.  The paper presents a formative study involving driver interviews to understand preferences for persuasion strategies. The resulting system uses GPT-4 to generate persuasive content via voice and UI elements, based on factors such as risk level and established persuasion principles. An experiment with 36 participants compares the LLM-based persuasion system with a baseline system that uses standard takeover request audio alerts.  The results suggest that the persuasion system reduces secondary task engagement, promotes better attention allocation (as measured by eye-tracking), lowers cognitive load, and is perceived more favorably by drivers in terms of comprehensibility and comfort.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in its application of LLMs for *proactive persuasion* in automated driving. While prior work has investigated the use of LLMs in driving contexts (e.g., as co-pilots, risk assessment), the specific focus on using LLMs to gently guide driver behavior away from distractions before emergencies arise is relatively new. The combination of eye-tracking for attention monitoring with LLM-generated persuasive content is also a novel integration. The use of various persuasion strategies guided by Fogg's model further adds to the novelty. However, the core idea of persuasion in driving is not completely novel; the novelty is in the *how* – using an LLM in a proactive, personalized way. The level of detail in system architecture implementation may be limited.

*Significance:*

The paper's significance stems from the potential to address a critical safety challenge in Level 3 automated driving: driver disengagement and delayed reactions during takeover requests. By demonstrating that an LLM can be used to proactively and subtly influence driver behavior, the research suggests a promising approach for improving safety and acceptance of automated vehicles. The finding that the persuasion system reduced cognitive load and improved attention allocation has important implications for driver well-being and safety performance. The user preferences for specific persuasive strategies provides valuable insights for future driver-assistance design. The detailed report on the impact on cognitive load makes this significant.

*Strengths:*

*   The paper addresses a practical and important problem in automated driving.
*   The use of LLMs to generate personalized and persuasive content is a promising approach.
*   The combination of eye-tracking and LLM-based intervention is well-motivated.
*   The experimental design is reasonably sound, with a comparison to a relevant baseline.
*   The subjective and objective metrics provide a comprehensive evaluation.
*   Results demonstrate the benefits of the persuasion system over the baseline.

*Weaknesses:*

*   The experimental scenario is a simulated environment, which may limit the generalizability of the results to real-world driving.
*   The sample size (36 participants) is relatively small, which may limit the statistical power of the findings.
*   Some elements are not extensively expanded upon. Details on LLM prompts, system implementation, and the specific content of the persuasive messages could be more thorough.
*   The long-term effects of the persuasion system on driver behavior are not explored.

*Overall Assessment:*

The paper presents a compelling and innovative approach to address a critical safety challenge in automated driving. The application of LLMs for proactive driver persuasion is a significant contribution to the field. The experimental results provide strong evidence that the proposed system can improve driver attention allocation, reduce cognitive load, and enhance perceived system acceptability.  While the study has limitations (simulated environment, relatively small sample size), the overall contribution is substantial. The work lays a good foundation for future research to explore the practical applications of LLM-based persuasion in real-world automated driving scenarios.

### Pruning Large Language Models by Identifying and Preserving Functional Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05239v1)
- **Authors**: Yiheng Liu, Junhao Ning, Sichen Xia, Xiaohui Gao, Ning Qiang, Bao Ge, Junwei Han, Xintao Hu
- **Abstract**: Structured pruning is one of the representative techniques for compressing large language models (LLMs) to reduce GPU memory consumption and accelerate inference speed. It offers significant practical value in improving the efficiency of LLMs in real-world applications. Current structured pruning methods typically rely on assessment of the importance of the structure units and pruning the units with less importance. Most of them overlooks the interaction and collaboration among artificial neurons that are crucial for the functionalities of LLMs, leading to a disruption in the macro functional architecture of LLMs and consequently a pruning performance degradation. Inspired by the inherent similarities between artificial neural networks and functional neural networks in the human brain, we alleviate this challenge and propose to prune LLMs by identifying and preserving functional networks within LLMs in this study. To achieve this, we treat an LLM as a digital brain and decompose the LLM into functional networks, analogous to identifying functional brain networks in neuroimaging data. Afterwards, an LLM is pruned by preserving the key neurons within these functional networks. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can successfully identify and locate functional networks and key neurons in LLMs, enabling efficient model pruning. Our code is available at https://github.com/WhatAboutMyStar/LLM_ACTIVATION.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a structured pruning method for Large Language Models (LLMs) inspired by functional brain networks in neuroscience. The core idea is to view an LLM as a "digital brain" and decompose it into functional networks analogous to those identified in human brain imaging data (fMRI). This is achieved by applying Independent Component Analysis (ICA) to the neuron signals of LLMs to discover coherent functional networks. The pruning process then focuses on preserving key neurons within these identified networks, aiming to minimize disruption to the overall functional architecture and improve pruning performance. The method is evaluated on multiple benchmark datasets and compared to existing pruning techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of neuroscientific methods (specifically, functional brain network analysis using ICA) to LLM pruning. This is a non-trivial adaptation of techniques commonly used in neuroimaging to a completely different domain. The concept of viewing an LLM as a "digital brain" and applying analogous analytical tools provides a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a crucial limitation of existing LLM pruning methods, which often overlook the interdependencies and collaborations between neurons crucial for maintaining functionality. By explicitly identifying and preserving functional networks, the proposed method aims to mitigate performance degradation during pruning. Empirical results suggest that this approach leads to better or comparable performance compared to existing structured pruning methods, especially in tasks that assess reasoning abilities. The exploration of a biologically plausible pruning approach is also significant for the future interpretability and design of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty in Approach:** Adapting ICA from neuroscience to LLM pruning provides a unique perspective.
    *   **Addressing a Limitation:** The paper directly tackles the problem of disrupted functional cohesion during pruning, a valid concern for many existing methods.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The method is evaluated on multiple datasets with comparisons to relevant baselines.
    *   **Inspiration for Future Research:** The work opens up avenues for exploring other brain-inspired techniques in LLM analysis and design.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **ICA as a Linear Method:** The paper acknowledges that ICA, being a linear method, might not fully capture the complex, non-linear relationships between neurons in LLMs.  This limits the method's ability to fully characterize LLMs network functional architecture. The experiments exploring non-linear analysis techniques would significantly strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Scalability to Larger Models**: The discussion on scalability mentions using layer-by-layer ICA to combat the exponential growth in the number of neurons; however, this approach may lose the interaction between neurons from different layers. It would be advantageous to have a discussion and possibly experiments to show the proposed method can scale to larger models, such as models containing billions of parameters.
    *   **Justification of ICA Selection:** The paper does not explicitly justify *why* ICA was selected as the best approach from cognitive neuroscience. There are other neuroimaging techniques such as graph-based methods or seed-based correlation analyses which might have been explored, or at least given reason for not being selected.
    *   **Ablation Studies**: More comprehensive ablation studies on the various choices made (e.g. different ICA implementations or settings, alternative choices of calibration dataset) will further solidify the claims.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM pruning research by encouraging the incorporation of insights from neuroscience. If the approach proves to be robust and scalable, it could become a valuable tool for compressing and deploying LLMs efficiently. The neuroscientific grounding could also lead to more interpretable and biologically plausible AI models in the future.

*   **Overall:**  This paper presents a novel and interesting approach to LLM pruning using neuroscience-inspired techniques.  While it has some weaknesses, the core idea is solid, well-executed, and holds promise for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper warrants a 7.5 due to its novelty in adapting neuroscientific principles to LLM pruning, addressing a key limitation in existing methods, and providing empirical validation. However, the limitations of ICA, the limited explanation for why it was selected, and some areas for more comprehensive ablation studies prevents it from receiving a higher score. The paper's inspiration from neuroscience adds a unique angle with the potential to impact future research in the intersection of AI and brain-inspired methods.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeBoost: Boosting Code LLMs by Squeezing Knowledge from Code Snippets with RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05242v1)
- **Authors**: Sijie Wang, Quanjiang Guo, Kai Zhao, Yawei Zhang, Xin Li, Xiang Li, Siqi Li, Rui She, Shangshu Yu, Wee Peng Tay
- **Abstract**: Code large language models (LLMs) have become indispensable tools for building efficient and automated coding pipelines. Existing models are typically post-trained using reinforcement learning (RL) from general-purpose LLMs using "human instruction-final answer" pairs, where the instructions are usually from manual annotations. However, collecting high-quality coding instructions is both labor-intensive and difficult to scale. On the other hand, code snippets are abundantly available from various sources. This imbalance presents a major bottleneck in instruction-based post-training. We propose CodeBoost, a post-training framework that enhances code LLMs purely from code snippets, without relying on human-annotated instructions. CodeBoost introduces the following key components: (1) maximum-clique curation, which selects a representative and diverse training corpus from code; (2) bi-directional prediction, which enables the model to learn from both forward and backward prediction objectives; (3) error-aware prediction, which incorporates learning signals from both correct and incorrect outputs; (4) heterogeneous augmentation, which diversifies the training distribution to enrich code semantics; and (5) heterogeneous rewarding, which guides model learning through multiple reward types including format correctness and execution feedback from both successes and failures. Extensive experiments across several code LLMs and benchmarks verify that CodeBoost consistently improves performance, demonstrating its effectiveness as a scalable and effective training pipeline.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "CodeBoost: Boosting Code LLMs by Squeezing Knowledge from Code Snippets with RL."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CodeBoost, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) based post-training framework designed to enhance the performance of Code Large Language Models (LLMs) using only raw code snippets, without relying on human-annotated instructions.  The framework incorporates several key components: (1) Maximum-clique curation to create a diverse and representative training corpus; (2) Bi-directional prediction, which allows the model to learn from both forward (execution output prediction) and backward (code completion) objectives; (3) Error-aware prediction, leveraging information from both successful and failed code executions; (4) Heterogeneous augmentation, diversifying the training data to enrich code semantics; and (5) Heterogeneous rewarding, which guides model learning through format correctness, stdout, and stderr correctness. The authors demonstrate through experiments on various Code LLMs and benchmarks that CodeBoost consistently improves performance, suggesting it's a scalable and effective training pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty in Problem Formulation:** The core idea of post-training Code LLMs solely on code snippets, bypassing the need for expensive and hard-to-scale human annotations, is a significant and practical contribution. This addresses a real bottleneck in current instruction-based training pipelines.
    * **Comprehensive Framework:** The components of CodeBoost (clique curation, bi-directional prediction, error-aware prediction, heterogeneous augmentation, and heterogeneous rewarding) are well-motivated and designed to address specific challenges in learning from raw code. Each component appears to contribute towards the overall improvement.
    * **Rigorous Experiments:**  The paper conducts extensive experiments on multiple Code LLMs (Qwen, Llama, Seed-Coder, Yi-Coder) and a variety of benchmarks (BigCodeBench, CRUXEval, MBPP, EvalPlus, LiveCodeBench), demonstrating the broad applicability and effectiveness of CodeBoost. The ablation studies are also well-designed and provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    * **Scalability and Instruction-Free Approach:**  A key advantage of CodeBoost is its scalability since code snippets are more readily available than human-annotated instruction data.  The instruction-free nature makes it appealing for continuous improvement and adaptation of Code LLMs.
    * **Clear and well-written:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the proposed methodology clearly.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental rather than Transformative:** While the combination of components is novel and effective, the individual components themselves (e.g., RL training, data augmentation) are not entirely groundbreaking. The paper assembles these existing techniques in a clever and targeted way for this specific problem, but it doesn't present truly revolutionary, fundamental advancements in any one area.
    * **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** The experiments focus on Python code.  The paper should address the generalizability of CodeBoost to other programming languages.  While the core concepts are likely transferable, the specific augmentation and reward strategies may require language-specific adaptations.
    * **Reliance on Execution Environment:**  The framework relies heavily on the ability to execute code snippets in a sandbox environment to obtain execution outputs (stdout, stderr).  This might be problematic for code that requires specific external dependencies or environments that are difficult to replicate in a standard sandbox.
    * **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While error-aware prediction is introduced, the paper doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the types of errors that CodeBoost is particularly effective at addressing or the kinds of code snippets it struggles with.
    * **Lack of Comparison to Other Code Snippet Approaches:** While the authors rightfully point to a lack of instruction-based methods using code snippets, the authors should mention/compare with code-completion or code summarization methods that may make use of related data.
    * **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions the use of 8 Tesla A100 GPUs and the training time, it would benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational cost of each component, especially the clique curation and code execution steps. This is important for assessing the practical feasibility of CodeBoost in resource-constrained environments.

* **Significance:**

CodeBoost presents a significant step towards more efficient and scalable training of Code LLMs.  The shift from reliance on human annotations to leveraging readily available code snippets is a valuable contribution with practical implications for the field.  The framework's effectiveness in improving the performance of existing Code LLMs across multiple benchmarks makes it a potentially influential technique.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7 out of 10.

* **Rationale:** The paper presents a well-designed and empirically validated framework that tackles a real-world problem in Code LLM training (the scarcity of high-quality instruction data). The comprehensive nature of the approach and the demonstrated improvements on multiple benchmarks make it a significant contribution to the field. However, the individual components are not entirely novel, and there are some limitations regarding generalizability, reliance on execution environments, and computational cost analysis. While it provides a practical and effective solution, it doesn't represent a groundbreaking theoretical or conceptual leap that would warrant a higher score. The novelty is primarily in the clever combination and application of existing techniques to a specific and important problem.

### Salt-Rock Creep Deformation Forecasting Using Deep Neural Networks and Analytical Models for Subsurface Energy Storage Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05248v1)
- **Authors**: Pradeep Kumar Shukla, Tanujit Chakraborty, Mustafa Sari, Joel Sarout, Partha Pratim Mandal
- **Abstract**: This study provides an in-depth analysis of time series forecasting methods to predict the time-dependent deformation trend (also known as creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressure conditions. Creep deformation assessment is essential for designing and operating underground storage facilities for nuclear waste, hydrogen energy, or radioactive materials. Salt rocks, known for their mechanical properties like low porosity, low permeability, high ductility, and exceptional creep and self-healing capacities, were examined using multi-stage triaxial (MSTL) creep data. After resampling, axial strain datasets were recorded at 5--10 second intervals under confining pressure levels ranging from 5 to 35 MPa over 5.8--21 days. Initial analyses, including Seasonal-Trend Decomposition (STL) and Granger causality tests, revealed minimal seasonality and causality between axial strain and temperature data. Further statistical tests, such as the Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) test, confirmed the stationarity of the data with p-values less than 0.05, and wavelet coherence plot (WCP) analysis indicated repeating trends. A suite of deep neural network (DNN) models (Neural Basis Expansion Analysis for Time Series (N-BEATS), Temporal Convolutional Networks (TCN), Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN), and Transformers (TF)) was utilized and compared against statistical baseline models. Predictive performance was evaluated using Root Mean Square Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), and Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (SMAPE). Results demonstrated that N-BEATS and TCN models outperformed others across various stress levels, respectively. DNN models, particularly N-BEATS and TCN, showed a 15--20\% improvement in accuracy over traditional analytical models, effectively capturing complex temporal dependencies and patterns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of deep neural networks (DNNs), specifically N-BEATS and TCN, for forecasting the time-dependent deformation (creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressures, a crucial aspect for designing and operating underground energy storage facilities. The study uses multi-stage triaxial (MSTL) creep data and compares the performance of DNN models against traditional analytical and statistical baseline models. Key findings include that DNN models, particularly N-BEATS and TCN, outperform traditional methods by 15-20% in accuracy, effectively capturing complex temporal dependencies.  The paper also analyzes the data for seasonality, causality, and stationarity before applying the models and uses several statistical metrics (RMSE, MAE, MAPE, SMAPE) and the Multiple Comparisons with the Best (MCB) test to validate the results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of N-BEATS and TCN architectures to the specific problem of salt rock creep forecasting, particularly with the depth of comparative analysis against a wide range of traditional methods, appears to be a significant contribution. However, time series forecasting in geomechanics is not entirely new, and LSTMs have been tried before; however, the comprehensive comparison and the use of very specific architectures like N-BEATS increase the novelty. The conformal predictions adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** Accurate creep prediction is critical for ensuring the long-term safety and stability of underground storage facilities (nuclear waste, hydrogen, etc.). Improvements in prediction accuracy, as claimed in this paper, could translate to more efficient designs and reduced risk of failure in these facilities. The improved accuracy (15-20% as stated) has the potential to affect safety and economy of underground salt cavern energy storage systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper systematically compares DNN models (N-BEATS, TCN, RNN, Transformer) with a wide range of traditional models (statistical and analytical), providing a holistic performance assessment.
    *   **Rigorous Validation:** Using multiple evaluation metrics (RMSE, MAE, MAPE, SMAPE) and statistical tests (STL, Granger Causality, ADF, MCB) strengthens the validity of the results. The use of Conformal Predictions is a strong aspect for error quantification.
    *   **Practical Application:** The research addresses a real-world problem with significant engineering and economic implications.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the experimental setup, data processing steps, and model implementation details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Details:** While the experimental setup is mentioned, more details about the specific salt rock used (origin, composition, etc.) would enhance the reproducibility and generalizability of the results.
    *   **Temperature Neglect:** While justified by statistical tests, ignoring temperature as an input variable in most analyses could be a limitation. Future studies should definitely explore multivariate models with temperature, humidity, confining pressure, deviatoric stress etc.
    *   **Overclaiming:** Some of the phrasing gives the impression that the method proposed provides accuracy superior to every other method in almost any scenarios; there is a lack of critical insight into when would the model be appropriate to use in relation to the other competing approaches.
    *   **Black Box Nature of DNN's** DNN are complex to understand, therefore, from a user standpoint there is a reduced insight about why the method work better or worse in a specific location
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could encourage wider adoption of DNN models for creep prediction in geomechanics. The comparative analysis provides a valuable benchmark for future research in this area. The MCB helps to strengthen the method, since a statistical analysis of the models used is provided. The paper serves as a valuable starting point for practitioners looking to incorporate DNNs into their workflow.
    * The conclusions would benefit from a section describing the limitations found during the research.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of advanced DNN models (particularly N-BEATS and TCN) for improving creep prediction in salt rock, a critical area for underground energy storage. The study's strength lies in its comprehensive comparison of various models, robust validation using multiple metrics and statistical tests. The use of Conformal Prediction adds depth, allowing the user to understand in which conditions the method is acceptable. However, it's crucial to acknowledge the paper's limitations, including the neglect of the temperature parameter in the analysis and the lack of detailed geological characterization of the tested salt rock, and some overclaiming. Also, there is a lack of interpretation about the internal working of DNN's from a user standpoint. Taking these points into consideration, the assigned score of 7 reflects the significant advancements brought by the paper, with room for improvement and future research to address the highlighted limitations and add even more value.

- **Classification**: physics.geo-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### MoBE: Mixture-of-Basis-Experts for Compressing MoE-based LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05257v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaodong Chen, Mingming Ha, Zhenzhong Lan, Jing Zhang, Jianguo Li
- **Abstract**: The Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has become a predominant paradigm for scaling large language models (LLMs). Despite offering strong performance and computational efficiency, large MoE-based LLMs like DeepSeek-V3-0324 and Kimi-K2-Instruct present serious challenges due to substantial memory requirements in deployment. While recent works have explored MoE compression to address this issue, existing methods often suffer from considerable accuracy drops (e.g., 7-14% relatively) even at modest compression rates. This paper introduces a novel Mixture-of-Basis-Experts (MoBE) method that achieves model compression while incurring minimal accuracy drops. Specifically, each up/gate matrix in an expert is decomposed via a rank decomposition as W = AB, where matrix A is unique to each expert. The relatively larger matrix B is further re-parameterized as a linear combination of basis matrices {Bi} shared across all experts within a given MoE layer. The factorization is learned by minimizing the reconstruction error relative to the original weight matrices. Experiments demonstrate that MoBE achieves notably lower accuracy drops compared to prior works. For instance, MoBE can reduce the parameter counts of Qwen3-235B-A22B-2507, DeepSeek-V3-0324 (671B) and Kimi-K2-Instruct (1T) by 24%-30% with only 1%-2% accuracy drop (about 2% drops when measured relatively).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mixture-of-Basis-Experts (MoBE), a novel method for compressing Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)-based Large Language Models (LLMs).  MoBE decomposes the up/gate matrix of each expert using a rank decomposition (W = AB). Matrix A is unique to each expert, while the larger matrix B is re-parameterized as a linear combination of shared basis matrices across all experts within a given MoE layer.  The factorization is learned by minimizing the reconstruction error relative to the original weight matrices.  Experimental results show that MoBE achieves lower accuracy drops compared to prior MoE compression methods, demonstrated on various LLMs such as Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-V3, and Kimi-K2, with around 1-2% accuracy drop while achieving 24%-30% parameter reduction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using shared basis matrices for MoE compression has some novelty, building upon existing decomposition techniques.  The decomposition approach, AB,  is not entirely novel in matrix factorization, but its application *within* the MoE expert structure, particularly combined with shared basis matrices, represents a meaningful contribution. Prior methods like MoLAE also use a decomposition and shared latent matrix, and D2-MoE extracts shared weights, so MoBE's innovation lies in how it combines rank decomposition with shared basis specifically. The paper's novelty score is reduced because of existing related methods. However, the experimental data demonstrate a marked improvement over existing methods.
*   **Significance:** Reducing the memory footprint of large MoE-based LLMs is a highly relevant problem for deployment.  The demonstrated ability to compress models with minimal accuracy loss is valuable.  The experimental results on leading open-source LLMs increase the practical impact, suggesting the MoBE method is effective across diverse models and sizes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides extensive experimental evidence, comparing MoBE against existing methods on multiple LLMs and benchmarks. This demonstrates the effectiveness of MoBE across diverse architectures and tasks. The consistent reduction in accuracy drops compared to MoLAE and D2-MoE is compelling.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the MoBE architecture and the training process. The algorithm provided also helps to recreate the results.
    *   **Address Important Problem:** The study directly tackles a significant problem hindering the deployment of large MoE models: excessive memory requirements.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Innovation:** While effective, the approach builds upon existing decomposition and sharing techniques for MoE compression. It's more of an evolution than a revolution.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a deep theoretical understanding of why MoBE works so well compared to other decomposition methods. A more thorough theoretical justification could strengthen the paper.
    *   **Ablation Study Could be Deeper:** While the ablation study on activation functions and Z-score normalization is helpful, further analysis exploring different rank values, basis matrix sizes, or different optimization strategies for training A and B could be explored.
    *   **Reliance on Adam:** While stated, Adam optimizer is utilized, and this may not always be the best approach. This should be evaluated across each model.

*   **Potential Impact:** MoBE has the potential to become a valuable tool for compressing and deploying large MoE-based LLMs. Wider adoption of MoBE could facilitate the use of these models in resource-constrained environments. The method is easy to implement and could influence future research in MoE compression, and has a possibility of changing how models are compressed.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to MoE model compression. While the innovation is not groundbreaking, the method demonstrates significant improvement over existing techniques through strong empirical validation. The paper addresses a real-world problem and may lead to practical advancements in LLM deployment. The identified weaknesses are avenues for future research to build upon this work.
