### Group Causal Policy Optimization for Post-Training Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05428v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyin Gu, Jingyao Wang, Ran Zuo, Chuxiong Sun, Zeen Song, Changwen Zheng, Wenwen Qiang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have broadened their applicability across diverse tasks, yet specialized domains still require targeted post training. Among existing methods, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) stands out for its efficiency, leveraging groupwise relative rewards while avoiding costly value function learning. However, GRPO treats candidate responses as independent, overlooking semantic interactions such as complementarity and contradiction. To address this challenge, we first introduce a Structural Causal Model (SCM) that reveals hidden dependencies among candidate responses induced by conditioning on a final integrated output forming a collider structure. Then, our causal analysis leads to two insights: (1) projecting responses onto a causally informed subspace improves prediction quality, and (2) this projection yields a better baseline than query only conditioning. Building on these insights, we propose Group Causal Policy Optimization (GCPO), which integrates causal structure into optimization through two key components: a causally informed reward adjustment and a novel KL regularization term that aligns the policy with a causally projected reference distribution. Comprehensive experimental evaluations demonstrate that GCPO consistently surpasses existing methods, including GRPO across multiple reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Group Causal Policy Optimization (GCPO), a novel post-training method for large language models (LLMs) designed to improve reasoning capabilities. GCPO builds upon Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) by explicitly modeling the causal relationships between candidate responses generated for the same query.  The key idea is that the generation process creates a collider structure where conditioning on the final integrated output induces dependencies between initially independent candidate responses.  GCPO incorporates this causal structure through a causally-adjusted reward mechanism and a novel KL-divergence regularization term. Experiments on math and code reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that GCPO consistently outperforms existing methods, including GRPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in its application of causal inference principles to the problem of post-training LLMs for reasoning.  While GRPO effectively leverages group-wise relative rewards, it treats candidate responses as independent. GCPO's recognition of the collider structure and the subsequent development of a causally-informed objective is a genuinely new approach.  The  causally adjusted reward and KL divergence regularization are also well-motivated and novel components tailored to address the identified causal dependencies.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its ability to improve the reasoning performance of LLMs, particularly in complex domains.  By explicitly modeling the relationships between candidate responses, GCPO can better leverage the information contained within the group, leading to more coherent and accurate outputs.  The improvements observed on math and code reasoning benchmarks are substantial and demonstrate the potential of this approach. Moreover, the paper provides a theoretical justification for its approach, strengthening its contribution. The approach addresses a real limitation in current post-training methods by focusing on improving the coherence of output choices for a specific task from an LLM, something often overlooked.

**Strengths:**

*   **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a clear and well-reasoned causal analysis, justifying the proposed approach.
*   **Well-Motivated Components:** The causally-adjusted reward and KL-divergence regularization are directly derived from the causal analysis and designed to address the identified limitations of existing methods.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on a variety of challenging benchmarks and demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art methods.
*   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies confirm the importance of both the causally-adjusted reward and KL-divergence regularization terms.
*   **Clear and well-written** The paper is well-structured and presented.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Overhead:** The method introduces increased computation, which can potentially limit its scalability.  Although the paper acknowledges this, a more detailed analysis of the computational cost compared to GRPO, particularly in terms of wall-clock time, would be beneficial. The results on that point were also included.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method introduces new hyperparameters (a and κ), which may require careful tuning. This could be a burden.
*   **Limited Scope:** While the results are compelling, the paper primarily focuses on math and code reasoning tasks. It would be valuable to explore the applicability of GCPO to other domains.

**Potential Influence:**

GCPO has the potential to influence future research in the following ways:

*   **Causality-Aware Reinforcement Learning:**  The paper demonstrates the value of incorporating causal principles into reinforcement learning for LLMs, opening up new avenues for research in this area.
*   **Group-Based Optimization:**  The paper provides a more sophisticated approach to group-based optimization, which could be applied to other problems in LLM training and inference.
*   **Improved Reasoning Performance:**  The paper contributes to the ongoing effort to improve the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, making them more useful for a wider range of applications.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations regarding computational cost and hyperparameter tuning, its strengths far outweigh its weaknesses. The causal analysis is novel and insightful, the proposed method is well-motivated and effective, and the experimental results are compelling.  The paper addresses an important limitation in existing methods and has the potential to significantly advance the field of LLM training and reasoning.

Score: 8. The paper is a significant contribution with a strong theoretical foundation and compelling empirical results. While further work is needed to address the computational overhead and explore its applicability to other domains, its current form is significant and novel.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### MyCulture: Exploring Malaysia's Diverse Culture under Low-Resource Language Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05429v1)
- **Authors**: Zhong Ken Hew, Jia Xin Low, Sze Jue Yang, Chee Seng chan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often exhibit cultural biases due to training data dominated by high-resource languages like English and Chinese. This poses challenges for accurately representing and evaluating diverse cultural contexts, particularly in low-resource language settings. To address this, we introduce MyCulture, a benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate LLMs on Malaysian culture across six pillars: arts, attire, customs, entertainment, food, and religion presented in Bahasa Melayu. Unlike conventional benchmarks, MyCulture employs a novel open-ended multiple-choice question format without predefined options, thereby reducing guessing and mitigating format bias. We provide a theoretical justification for the effectiveness of this open-ended structure in improving both fairness and discriminative power. Furthermore, we analyze structural bias by comparing model performance on structured versus free-form outputs, and assess language bias through multilingual prompt variations. Our evaluation across a range of regional and international LLMs reveals significant disparities in cultural comprehension, highlighting the urgent need for culturally grounded and linguistically inclusive benchmarks in the development and assessment of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *MyCulture*, a new benchmark designed to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) on their understanding of Malaysian culture. The benchmark is presented in Bahasa Melayu and consists of over 2,600 questions covering six cultural domains: arts, attire, customs, entertainment, food, and religion. A key feature of *MyCulture* is the use of open-ended multiple-choice questions (MCQs) without predefined answer options. The authors argue that this format reduces guessing and format bias compared to traditional closed-form MCQs. The paper evaluates several regional and international LLMs on *MyCulture* and highlights significant disparities in cultural comprehension, emphasizing the need for culturally grounded benchmarks. The authors also analyze structural bias and language bias by varying the prompt language and comparing model performance on structured versus free-form outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of a Malaysia-specific cultural benchmark with an emphasis on open-ended MCQs. While there are existing cultural benchmarks, most focus on single-nation, culturally homogeneous settings. *MyCulture* addresses the multi-ethnic and multi-religious complexities of Malaysia. The open-ended MCQ format is a noteworthy attempt to mitigate the limitations of closed-form evaluations, though open-ended evaluation is explored in other works. The formal analysis to justify the advantage of the open-ended approach in terms of hardness and information-theoretic analysis is appreciated.

*Significance:*

The significance of the paper is in highlighting the limitations of LLMs in understanding and representing diverse cultural contexts, particularly in low-resource language settings. The results demonstrate that LLMs trained primarily on English and Chinese exhibit cultural biases, which can lead to culturally detached responses and exacerbate social exclusion. The paper underscores the need for culturally grounded benchmarks to ensure that LLMs are aligned with local values and practices. It also emphasizes the importance of using open-ended evaluation methods to accurately assess model performance in real-world scenarios.

*Strengths:*

*   **Culturally grounded and specific:** The benchmark is carefully designed to reflect the nuances of Malaysian culture.
*   **Open-ended MCQ format:** The use of open-ended MCQs is a novel approach to reduce guessing and format bias.
*   **Thorough evaluation:** The paper evaluates a range of regional and international LLMs.
*   **Bias analysis:** The paper analyzes structural and language bias.
*   **Detailed data analysis:** Theoretical analysis on both closed-form vs open-ended is robust.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Low-resource language:** Focusing on Malay is crucial yet may be influenced from translation from high-resource languages to low-resource languages during dataset preparation.

*   **Scalability and Evaluation Complexity of Open-ended questions:** It typically requires human verification and judging the correctness of the answer.
*   **Limited exploration of mitigation strategies:** While the paper highlights the problem of cultural bias, it does not offer concrete strategies for mitigating this bias.
*   **Generalizability to other diverse cultural contexts:** The benchmark is specific to Malaysia, which limits its generalizability to other diverse cultural contexts. However, the methodology is generalizable.

*Impact:*

The paper has the potential to influence the development of more culturally sensitive and inclusive LLMs. By highlighting the limitations of existing models and the importance of culturally grounded benchmarks, it can encourage researchers and developers to prioritize cultural alignment in their work. The *MyCulture* benchmark can serve as a valuable resource for evaluating LLMs on Malaysian culture and promoting linguistic diversity.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by introducing a culturally grounded benchmark for evaluating LLMs in a diverse and low-resource context. The open-ended MCQ format is a novel approach to reduce guessing and format bias. However, the paper has some limitations, such as focusing on Malay which could be influenced by translating from high-resource languages, difficulty in evaluating open-ended questions, and does not offer concrete mitigation strategies, and the lack of direct comparison to existing benchmarks. Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contribution and the potential for further research in this area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Discovering Interpretable Programmatic Policies via Multimodal LLM-assisted Evolutionary Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05433v1)
- **Authors**: Qinglong Hu, Xialiang Tong, Mingxuan Yuan, Fei Liu, Zhichao Lu, Qingfu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Interpretability and high performance are essential goals in designing control policies, particularly for safety-critical tasks. Deep reinforcement learning has greatly enhanced performance, yet its inherent lack of interpretability often undermines trust and hinders real-world deployment. This work addresses these dual challenges by introducing a novel approach for programmatic policy discovery, called Multimodal Large Language Model-assisted Evolutionary Search (MLES). MLES utilizes multimodal large language models as policy generators, combining them with evolutionary mechanisms for automatic policy optimization. It integrates visual feedback-driven behavior analysis within the policy generation process to identify failure patterns and facilitate targeted improvements, enhancing the efficiency of policy discovery and producing adaptable, human-aligned policies. Experimental results show that MLES achieves policy discovery capabilities and efficiency comparable to Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) across two control tasks, while offering transparent control logic and traceable design processes. This paradigm overcomes the limitations of predefined domain-specific languages, facilitates knowledge transfer and reuse, and is scalable across various control tasks. MLES shows promise as a leading approach for the next generation of interpretable control policy discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces Multimodal LLM-assisted Evolutionary Search (MLES), a novel framework for automatically discovering interpretable programmatic policies for control tasks.  MLES leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) within an evolutionary search loop to generate and refine policies.  Key innovations include (1) directly synthesizing executable policies with natural language rationales, and (2) integrating visual feedback from policy executions into the evolutionary process, which allows the system to improve policies more effectively by identifying failure modes. The paper demonstrates MLES's capabilities on Lunar Lander and Car Racing, showing that it achieves performance comparable to Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) while producing transparent control logic and traceable design processes.  The authors also explore how different forms of visual behavior evidence and prompt design influence policy evolution.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for policy *generation* is present in the landscape of research utilizing LLMs for solving control tasks. Existing LLM-based works usually focus on shaping reward function or high-level planning. MLES differs by directly learning interpretable and human-aligned policies. The incorporation of visual feedback and behavior analysis into the evolution loop to facilitate policy refinement is the more significant novel component.  While the overall architecture follows the general LES paradigm, the integration of visual feedback and interpretable behavior evidence tailored to the specific task significantly enhances the search efficiency and the quality of generated policies. Therefore, the approach represents a meaningful advancement, but not a radical departure, from existing methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in bridging the gap between high performance and interpretability in control. DRL excels in performance but often lacks transparency, hindering trust and deployment, especially in safety-critical applications. MLES offers a promising path towards more trustworthy AI systems by producing policies that are both effective and understandable. The modularity of MLES could also allow human experts to intervene and refine the policy discovery process, ensuring that the resultant policies align with human values and expectations. This would have broad implications for robotics, autonomous systems, and other control-related fields where transparency is paramount.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Interpretability:**  A major strength is the focus on directly synthesizing *interpretable* programmatic policies.  This contrasts with many DRL methods that rely on black-box neural networks.
    *   **Visual Feedback Integration:** The incorporation of visual feedback into the evolutionary loop allows MLES to identify and address failure modes, leading to more robust and adaptable policies.
    *   **Performance:** The paper demonstrates that MLES can achieve performance comparable to PPO on standard benchmarks.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The authors provide a good analysis of the impact of different IBE formats and prompt designs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scalability:** The reliance on MLLMs could limit scalability to more complex, high-dimensional control tasks.  Querying MLLMs can be computationally expensive, which restricts the search space that can be explored within a reasonable time frame.
    *   **Generalization:** While the paper discusses generalization and performs some initial experiments with policy ensembling, it acknowledges that generalization remains a challenge.
    *   **Dependency on MLLMs:** The success of MLES is tightly coupled to the capabilities of the underlying MLLMs. This is a double-edged sword. It leverages the rapid progress in LLMs, but also makes the framework vulnerable to limitations and biases of these models.
    *   **Manual Effort:**  Although the approach aims for automated policy design, the current implementation of MLES requires manual effort in designing interpretable behavioral evidence, including constructing prompts. The paper proposed potential solutions (video-based and automatic IBE design) in future works, showing that these manual efforts might be relieved, but have not been shown yet.
    *   **Choice of Baselines:** The choice of DQN as a baseline for a continuous control problem like Car Racing is not ideal. While PPO is used as another baseline, a more modern on-policy algorithm (like SAC) may have strengthened the comparisons.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the scalability and generalization issues can be addressed, MLES could significantly influence the field of interpretable reinforcement learning and control. It offers a compelling alternative to black-box DRL approaches and could facilitate the development of more trustworthy and human-aligned AI systems.

**Justification for Score**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper presents a novel and valuable approach to interpretable policy discovery.  The integration of visual feedback and direct synthesis of programmatic policies are significant contributions. The experimental results are promising, demonstrating performance comparable to PPO on standard benchmarks. However, the limitations related to scalability, generalization, the manual design of behavioral evidence, and dependence on MLLM capabilities prevent a higher score. While the innovation on top of standard LES is incremental, the impact for safety-critical tasks, where interpretability is of paramount importance, is substantial.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMEval-3: A Large-Scale Longitudinal Study on Robust and Fair Evaluation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05452v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Zhang, Yujiong Shen, Jingyi Deng, Yuhui Wang, Yue Zhang, Junzhe Wang, Shichun Liu, Shihan Dou, Huayu Sha, Qiyuan Peng, Changhao Jiang, Jingqi Tong, Yilong Wu, Zhihao Zhang, Mingqi Wu, Zhiheng Xi, Mingxu Chai, Tao Liang, Zhihui Fei, Zhen Wang, Mingyang Wan, Guojun Ma, Tao Gui, Qi Zhang, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Existing evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) on static benchmarks is vulnerable to data contamination and leaderboard overfitting, critical issues that obscure true model capabilities. To address this, we introduce LLMEval-3, a framework for dynamic evaluation of LLMs. LLMEval-3 is built on a proprietary bank of 220k graduate-level questions, from which it dynamically samples unseen test sets for each evaluation run. Its automated pipeline ensures integrity via contamination-resistant data curation, a novel anti-cheating architecture, and a calibrated LLM-as-a-judge process achieving 90% agreement with human experts, complemented by a relative ranking system for fair comparison. An 20-month longitudinal study of nearly 50 leading models reveals a performance ceiling on knowledge memorization and exposes data contamination vulnerabilities undetectable by static benchmarks. The framework demonstrates exceptional robustness in ranking stability and consistency, providing strong empirical validation for the dynamic evaluation paradigm. LLMEval-3 offers a robust and credible methodology for assessing the true capabilities of LLMs beyond leaderboard scores, promoting the development of more trustworthy evaluation standards.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the LLMEval-3 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLMEval-3, a dynamic evaluation framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) designed to address the issues of data contamination and leaderboard overfitting that plague static benchmarks. LLMEval-3 employs a proprietary bank of over 220k graduate-level questions, dynamically sampled for each evaluation run, along with an anti-cheating architecture and a calibrated LLM-as-a-judge system. A 20-month longitudinal study of nearly 50 LLMs using this framework reveals performance ceilings, exposes previously undetectable data contamination, and demonstrates robust ranking stability and consistency.  The authors argue that LLMEval-3 offers a more credible and trustworthy methodology for assessing LLM capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Critical Problem:** The paper tackles a significant and well-recognized problem in the LLM evaluation landscape: the unreliability of static benchmarks due to data contamination and overfitting.
*   **Dynamic Evaluation:** The dynamic sampling of questions from a private bank is a substantial improvement over static benchmarks, making the evaluation process much more resistant to data contamination and strategic manipulation.
*   **Anti-Cheating Architecture:** The secure two-layer anti-cheating architecture strengthens the reliability of the benchmark by minimizing risks of manipulation and ensuring the integrity of the evaluation process.
*   **LLM-as-a-Judge Calibration:** The meticulous calibration and validation of the LLM-as-a-judge system, achieving high human-machine agreement, provides a cost-effective and scalable alternative to human evaluation.
*   **Longitudinal Study:**  The extensive longitudinal study (20 months, nearly 50 models, 150k+ evaluation data points) offers a wealth of empirical data and provides a more comprehensive understanding of LLM performance trends and limitations than many cross-sectional studies.
*   **Empirical Findings:** The findings (performance ceiling, domain-specific performance variations, and limitations of prompting) provide new insights into the capabilities and limitations of current LLMs.
*   **Robustness Validation:**  The experiments validating ranking stability with multi-round resampling and comparisons to Elo ranking demonstrate the robustness and reliability of the LLMEval-3 framework.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Proprietary Data:**  The reliance on a proprietary question bank, while necessary for preventing data contamination, makes it difficult for other researchers to independently verify and reproduce the results. This lack of transparency could raise concerns about potential biases in the question bank.
*   **Geographic and Educational Bias:** The graduate-level questions are sourced exclusively from Chinese universities. This introduces a significant geographical and educational bias, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings to other cultural and educational contexts. Is the 'knowledge' in the questions representative of a global graduate-level curriculum?
*   **LLM-as-a-Judge Limitations:** While the LLM-as-a-judge system is well-calibrated, it is still inherently limited by the capabilities and biases of the underlying LLM (GPT-4o in this case). Even with high human-machine agreement, subtle differences in evaluation criteria may exist.
*   **Limited Scope of Error Analysis:** While error analysis is performed, the paper does not go into as much detail about the *types* of questions or specific areas of weakness beyond broad domain categorizations.  A deeper dive into error patterns would increase the paper's value.
*   **Lack of Comparative Cost Analysis:** Although the paper claims that the LLM-as-a-Judge approach provides more cost-effective advantages than human evaluation, there is a lack of comparative cost analysis and a clear quantitative justification.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The dynamic evaluation framework with a private dataset and anti-cheating mechanisms is a notable advancement in the field. The longitudinal study and findings regarding performance ceilings, data contamination, and ranking stability contribute significantly to the understanding of LLM capabilities and limitations. The LLMEval-3 approach provides a more robust and trustworthy assessment of LLMs. It is important to consider the geographically limited scope of the data. A follow-up study using a more diverse dataset is needed.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a compelling and well-executed approach to addressing a critical issue in LLM evaluation. The LLMEval-3 framework offers substantial improvements over static benchmarks, and the longitudinal study provides valuable empirical insights. However, the proprietary nature of the question bank, the geographic bias, and some limitations on error analysis and cost comparison slightly detract from the overall impact. Despite these limitations, the paper is a significant contribution to the field. It provides a more reliable and trustworthy method for assessing LLMs, pushing forward the development of more rigorous evaluation standards. The authors' work will influence future research on LLM evaluation methodologies and promote the development of more trustworthy LLM evaluation standards.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### EnergyPatchTST: Multi-scale Time Series Transformers with Uncertainty Estimation for Energy Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05454v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Li, Zixin Wang, Qizheng Sun, Qixiang Gao, Fenglei Yang
- **Abstract**: Accurate and reliable energy time series prediction is of great significance for power generation planning and allocation. At present, deep learning time series prediction has become the mainstream method. However, the multi-scale time dynamics and the irregularity of real data lead to the limitations of the existing methods. Therefore, we propose EnergyPatchTST, which is an extension of the Patch Time Series Transformer specially designed for energy forecasting. The main innovations of our method are as follows: (1) multi-scale feature extraction mechanism to capture patterns with different time resolutions; (2) probability prediction framework to estimate uncertainty through Monte Carlo elimination; (3) integration path of future known variables (such as temperature and wind conditions); And (4) Pre-training and Fine-tuning examples to enhance the performance of limited energy data sets. A series of experiments on common energy data sets show that EnergyPatchTST is superior to other commonly used methods, the prediction error is reduced by 7-12%, and reliable uncertainty estimation is provided, which provides an important reference for time series prediction in the energy field.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation with a score for the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "EnergyPatchTST: Multi-scale Time Series Transformers with Uncertainty Estimation for Energy Forecasting" introduces a novel deep learning model for energy time series forecasting.  The model, EnergyPatchTST, builds upon the PatchTST architecture and incorporates several key enhancements: (1) a multi-scale feature extraction mechanism to capture patterns at different time resolutions, (2) a probability prediction framework based on Monte Carlo dropout for uncertainty estimation, (3) a pathway for integrating future known variables (e.g., weather forecasts), and (4) a pre-training and fine-tuning strategy to improve performance on limited energy data.  The authors demonstrate that EnergyPatchTST outperforms existing methods on common energy datasets, achieving reduced prediction error and providing reliable uncertainty estimates. The paper concludes that the proposed model has potential for real-world applications in energy forecasting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multi-Scale Approach:** The use of multi-scale feature extraction is a relevant and well-motivated approach for energy time series, which inherently exhibit dynamics at different temporal granularities (short-term fluctuations, daily/weekly patterns, seasonal trends). This is a good insight and likely contributes significantly to the performance gains.
    *   **Uncertainty Estimation:** The integration of Monte Carlo dropout for uncertainty estimation is a strong addition, addressing a crucial need in energy forecasting where reliable prediction intervals are important for risk management and decision-making. Probabilistic forecasting enhances model robustness.
    *   **Future Variable Integration:** Incorporating known future variables like weather forecasts directly addresses a key dependency in energy forecasting and provides a practical advantage.
    *   **Pre-training and Fine-tuning:** The use of transfer learning (pre-training and fine-tuning) is beneficial, particularly in scenarios where energy datasets are limited, enabling the model to leverage broader time-series patterns.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments using real-world energy datasets, comparing against a strong set of baselines (Informer, Autoformer, FEDformer, PatchTST, etc.). Ablation studies are well-designed and provide insights into the contribution of individual components. The use of evaluation metrics relevant to both point forecasting and probabilistic forecasting (MSE, CRPS, PI-Coverage) is appropriate.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly presents the model architecture, methodology, and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of features is well-engineered, the individual components of EnergyPatchTST (multi-scale, uncertainty estimation via MC dropout, future variable integration) are not entirely novel on their own. They are known techniques applied within the context of PatchTST for energy forecasting. The novelty lies in the *integration* and adaptation of these techniques for this specific application domain.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the ablation study is useful, a deeper theoretical analysis of the learned features and uncertainty estimates would be beneficial. A more in-depth analysis of the scale analysis findings would also improve the paper.
    *   **Limited Focus on Interpretability:** While the multi-scale approach offers some inherent interpretability, the paper could further investigate and explain the model's learned representations and predictions in a more transparent manner.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** The paper does not adequately address the computational overhead associated with the multi-scale processing and Monte Carlo dropout. This is an important consideration for real-world deployment, and a discussion of the model's efficiency would be valuable.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem in the energy domain (accurate and reliable forecasting) and offers a solution that is directly applicable to real-world scenarios.
    *   **Performance Improvement:** The reported performance improvements over existing methods are significant (7-12% error reduction) and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Community Contribution:** The paper's findings and the proposed EnergyPatchTST model contribute to the growing body of research on deep learning for time series forecasting, particularly in the energy domain.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field of energy forecasting. While the individual components are not ground-breakingly novel, their intelligent integration and adaptation within the PatchTST architecture, combined with thorough experimental validation, make it a valuable addition. The practical relevance and significant performance improvements justify a positive evaluation. The weaknesses mainly revolve around the depth of analysis and a lack of discussion on computational complexity. The work is more of an evolutionary improvement than a revolutionary one.

### TASE: Token Awareness and Structured Evaluation for Multilingual Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05468v1)
- **Authors**: Chenzhuo Zhao, Xinda Wang, Yue Huang, Junting Lu, Ziqian Liu
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance on high-level semantic tasks, they often struggle with fine-grained, token-level understanding and structural reasoning--capabilities that are essential for applications requiring precision and control. We introduce TASE, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate LLMs' ability to perceive and reason about token-level information across languages. TASE covers 10 tasks under two core categories: token awareness and structural understanding, spanning Chinese, English, and Korean, with a 35,927-instance evaluation set and a scalable synthetic data generation pipeline for training. Tasks include character counting, token alignment, syntactic structure parsing, and length constraint satisfaction. We evaluate over 30 leading commercial and open-source LLMs, including O3, Claude 4, Gemini 2.5 Pro, and DeepSeek-R1, and train a custom Qwen2.5-14B model using the GRPO training method. Results show that human performance significantly outpaces current LLMs, revealing persistent weaknesses in token-level reasoning. TASE sheds light on these limitations and provides a new diagnostic lens for future improvements in low-level language understanding and cross-lingual generalization. Our code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/cyzcz/Tase .
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TASE: Token Awareness and Structured Evaluation for Multilingual Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TASE, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the token-level understanding and structural reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across multiple languages (English, Chinese, and Korean). TASE consists of ten fine-grained tasks spanning two categories: token awareness (e.g., counting tokens, reordering) and structural understanding (e.g., character counting, visual token recognition). The authors curate a dataset of 35,928 evaluation instances and develop a scalable synthetic data generation pipeline for training and fine-tuning models. They evaluate over 30 leading LLMs, including proprietary and open-source models, and demonstrate that even state-of-the-art LLMs struggle with TASE tasks, particularly those requiring structure-aware reasoning.  The authors also show that while targeted fine-tuning improves performance, a significant gap remains between human and model capabilities in token-level understanding.  The paper emphasizes the importance of token-level understanding and structural reasoning for applications requiring precision and control and argues that current benchmarks largely overlook these capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the creation of a novel benchmark, TASE, specifically designed to assess token-level awareness and structural reasoning in LLMs. While some existing benchmarks touch on these abilities, TASE provides a comprehensive and systematic evaluation framework across multiple languages, a significant improvement. The benchmark focuses on a relatively unexplored aspect of LLM capabilities, which constitutes a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical weakness in current LLMs. While LLMs excel at high-level semantic tasks, they often struggle with more basic, fine-grained tasks that require accurate token manipulation and structural understanding. This deficiency has implications for various applications, such as tasks requiring precise text editing, code generation, or information retrieval in non-English languages. The benchmark provides a valuable tool for diagnosing these limitations and guiding future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** TASE offers a well-defined set of tasks covering various aspects of token awareness and structural reasoning.
    *   **Multilingual Scope:** The inclusion of English, Chinese, and Korean is a strength, allowing for cross-lingual analysis and revealing biases associated with different linguistic structures.
    *   **Scalable Data Generation:** The synthetic data generation pipeline ensures a reliable supply of training data and enables controlled experiments.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The evaluation of over 30 leading LLMs provides a thorough assessment of the current landscape.
    *   **Demonstration of Improvement through Fine-tuning**: GRPO based fine-tuning is shown to enhance the performance of existing models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task Complexity:** While the core idea is strong, the tasks, though fine-grained, are relatively simple and may not fully capture the nuances of real-world scenarios requiring token-level understanding.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** Although the GRPO method with synthesized data enables performance gains, it may be difficult to translate to other tasks/datasets.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The primary metric, accuracy, might not fully capture the spectrum of potential errors or subtle differences in performance. Error analysis and nuanced scoring functions could provide a more detailed understanding.
    *   **Dependency on Tokenizer**: The paper mentions that tokenizers affect results, it stops short of proposing or implementing novel tokenization solutions. It might be a good idea to also benchmark/show the effectiveness of tokenizer independent approaches to tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** TASE has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   **Guiding future research:** By identifying a critical weakness in LLMs, TASE can direct research efforts towards improving token-level understanding and structural reasoning.
    *   **Enabling better benchmarks:** TASE can be used as a building block for creating even more challenging benchmarks that require complex, low-level reasoning abilities.
    *   **Developing more robust LLMs:** By training and evaluating LLMs on TASE, researchers can develop models that are more robust and reliable for applications requiring precise text manipulation.

*Justification for Score:*

While the paper provides a valuable benchmark and insights, the task complexity and limited Generalization potential of GRPO based fine-tuning temper its overall contribution. The paper's real strength lies in illuminating a less explored dimension of language model capability and in offering a standardized, scalable mechanism for analyzing this area. Therefore, a score that reflects the strong potential while acknowledging limitations is warranted.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Large Language Models Generate Effective Datasets for Emotion Recognition in Conversations?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05474v1)
- **Authors**: Burak Can Kaplan, Hugo Cesar De Castro Carneiro, Stefan Wermter
- **Abstract**: Emotion recognition in conversations (ERC) focuses on identifying emotion shifts within interactions, representing a significant step toward advancing machine intelligence. However, ERC data remains scarce, and existing datasets face numerous challenges due to their highly biased sources and the inherent subjectivity of soft labels. Even though Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated their quality in many affective tasks, they are typically expensive to train, and their application to ERC tasks--particularly in data generation--remains limited. To address these challenges, we employ a small, resource-efficient, and general-purpose LLM to synthesize ERC datasets with diverse properties, supplementing the three most widely used ERC benchmarks. We generate six novel datasets, with two tailored to enhance each benchmark. We evaluate the utility of these datasets to (1) supplement existing datasets for ERC classification, and (2) analyze the effects of label imbalance in ERC. Our experimental results indicate that ERC classifier models trained on the generated datasets exhibit strong robustness and consistently achieve statistically significant performance improvements on existing ERC benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of small, general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate effective datasets for Emotion Recognition in Conversations (ERC).  Addressing the scarcity and bias in existing ERC datasets, the authors generate six novel datasets (natural and balanced) tailored to supplement three widely used ERC benchmarks (MELD, EmoryNLP, IEMOCAP). The paper evaluates the utility of these synthesized datasets by (1) augmenting existing datasets for ERC classification and (2) analyzing the impact of label imbalance. Experimental results demonstrate that ERC classifiers trained on the generated data exhibit strong robustness and consistently improve performance on existing benchmarks.  The authors share their prompts, code, and parameters to ensure reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to generating ERC datasets using *small*, open-source LLMs and demonstrating their utility for improving existing benchmarks. While using LLMs for data augmentation in NLP is not entirely novel, the specific application to ERC, the detailed methodology for generating different types of datasets (natural vs. balanced), and the thorough evaluation across multiple datasets and architectures contribute to its distinctiveness.  The focus on resource-efficient LLMs is particularly important given the prohibitive cost of API-based or large-scale model training.  The methodology for prompting and controlling the LLM to produce structured and consistent data for ERC tasks is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant bottleneck in ERC research: the lack of high-quality, diverse, and balanced datasets. The ability to generate synthetic datasets allows for more efficient experimentation and potentially reduces reliance on expensive and biased real-world data. The demonstrated performance improvements on existing benchmarks highlight the practical value of this approach. The open-sourcing of the code and parameters further enhances its significance by allowing other researchers to replicate and build upon this work. The work demonstrates the potential of even *smaller* LLMs for this application, opening the door for wider adoption of the technique. Analyzing the effect of different label distributions sheds light on benchmark-specific data requirements which allows data generation to be more targeted.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a critical issue in ERC (data scarcity and bias) and proposes a targeted solution.
    * **Thorough Methodology:** The data generation process is well-documented, including prompt engineering, LLM parameter settings, and dataset splitting strategies.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates the generated datasets using multiple ERC architectures and benchmarks, providing statistically significant results.
    * **Reproducibility:** The open-sourcing of code and parameters significantly enhances the reproducibility and impact of the research.
    * **Analysis of Label Distribution:** The analysis of balanced vs natural datasets provides insight into benchmark-specific dataset characteristics.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited LLM Exploration:** The paper primarily focuses on one LLM (Vicuna-13B). While justifiable from a resource perspective, exploring other small LLMs could broaden the scope of the findings. Although prompted, a deeper analysis into the 'reasoning' or understanding of 'emotion' by the LLM in relation to the generated dialogues would have been a welcome addition.
    * **Synthetic vs. Real Data Discrepancies:** While the experiments demonstrate improved performance, the gap between synthetic and real-world data remains a concern. Further investigation into the transferability of models trained on synthetic data to more diverse real-world scenarios would be valuable. The evaluation mainly uses textual data, ignoring the audio and visual aspects of the original datasets. It would be interesting to see how well this data can transfer to other modalities.
    * **Potential for Bias Amplification:** The LLM could potentially amplify biases present in its training data, leading to skewed emotion representation in the generated datasets. While the 'balanced' dataset attempts to mitigate this, a more rigorous analysis of potential bias amplification is necessary.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach could significantly influence ERC research by providing a cost-effective and scalable way to generate training data. It could also inspire the development of new techniques for controlling and evaluating LLMs for data augmentation in other NLP tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite some minor weaknesses, the paper presents a valuable and novel contribution to the field of ERC. The thorough methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and the potential for reproducibility make it a significant step forward in addressing data scarcity issues. The emphasis on using small, resource-efficient LLMs is particularly commendable.

### InfiAlign: A Scalable and Sample-Efficient Framework for Aligning LLMs to Enhance Reasoning Capabilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05496v1)
- **Authors**: Shuo Cai, Su Lu, Qi Zhou, Kejing Yang, Zhijie Sang, Congkai Xie, Hongxia Yang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have exhibited impressive reasoning abilities on a wide range of complex tasks. However, enhancing these capabilities through post-training remains resource intensive, particularly in terms of data and computational cost. Although recent efforts have sought to improve sample efficiency through selective data curation, existing methods often rely on heuristic or task-specific strategies that hinder scalability. In this work, we introduce InfiAlign, a scalable and sample-efficient post-training framework that integrates supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align LLMs for enhanced reasoning. At the core of InfiAlign is a robust data selection pipeline that automatically curates high-quality alignment data from open-source reasoning datasets using multidimensional quality metrics. This pipeline enables significant performance gains while drastically reducing data requirements and remains extensible to new data sources. When applied to the Qwen2.5-Math-7B-Base model, our SFT model achieves performance on par with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, while using only approximately 12% of the training data, and demonstrates strong generalization across diverse reasoning tasks. Additional improvements are obtained through the application of DPO, with particularly notable gains in mathematical reasoning tasks. The model achieves an average improvement of 3.89% on AIME 24/25 benchmarks. Our results highlight the effectiveness of combining principled data selection with full-stage post-training, offering a practical solution for aligning large reasoning models in a scalable and data-efficient manner. The model checkpoints are available at https://huggingface.co/InfiX-ai/InfiAlign-Qwen-7B-SFT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "InfiAlign: A Scalable and Sample-Efficient Framework for Aligning LLMs to Enhance Reasoning Capabilities":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InfiAlign, a framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through a sample-efficient and scalable post-training process. It combines Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with a robust data selection pipeline.  The data selection pipeline employs multi-dimensional metrics to identify high-quality alignment data from open-source resources, considering diversity, difficulty, and overall quality. The authors demonstrate that InfiAlign, when applied to the Qwen2.5-Math-7B-Base model, can achieve performance comparable to more data-intensive distilled models like DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B while using significantly less training data. They also show further improvements with DPO, especially in mathematical reasoning tasks, as evidenced by gains on the AIME benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of existing techniques (SFT, DPO) with a data selection pipeline that emphasizes a multi-dimensional approach to data quality.  While selective data curation is not entirely new, the systematic integration of diversity, difficulty, and quality metrics in an automated pipeline offers a practical improvement over reliance on heuristics or task-specific strategies.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in LLM alignment: the high resource requirements (data and compute) for enhancing reasoning capabilities. By demonstrating a significant reduction in data needs while maintaining or improving performance, InfiAlign offers a more accessible and sustainable path for LLM development, particularly for researchers and practitioners with limited resources. The ability to scale and adapt the framework to new data sources is also a valuable contribution. The modular approach is more novel compared to the model training, because there are only a few novel models these days.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The core strength is the demonstrated sample efficiency, achieving competitive results with a fraction of the data used by baseline models.
    *   **Scalability:** The modular design and automated data selection pipeline contribute to the framework's scalability and adaptability to new domains and data sources.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation across a diverse set of reasoning benchmarks, including mathematical, coding, scientific, and general knowledge tasks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of the data selection pipeline (e.g., diversity, difficulty), supporting the design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Training Strategies:** The SFT and DPO components are relatively standard.  The primary contribution is in the data selection pipeline, and the paper could benefit from exploring more novel training techniques in conjunction with the refined dataset.
    *   **Dependency on Open-Source Datasets:** While leveraging open-source data is practical, the performance is ultimately limited by the quality and diversity of these datasets.  Further research could explore how to augment InfiAlign with active learning or synthetic data generation to overcome these limitations.
    *   **Fine-tuning may be required for different dataset types**: The manually defined metrics may need tuning for unseen domains.

*   **Impact:** The InfiAlign framework has the potential to lower the barrier to entry for improving LLM reasoning abilities.  It offers a practical and resource-conscious approach that could be widely adopted by the research community and industry practitioners. By making alignment more accessible, it could accelerate progress in developing more capable and robust LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

InfiAlign is a well-executed and practically significant contribution. The core idea of multi-dimensional data selection for efficient LLM alignment is valuable and the comprehensive experiments support the effectiveness of the framework. While the individual components (SFT, DPO) are not groundbreaking, the integration of a robust data selection pipeline with these techniques is innovative and impactful. The paper demonstrates strong empirical results and provides valuable insights into the importance of data quality and diversity. Some more innovative data selection techniques could potentially improve the novelty/significance of the work. The weaknesses, such as the reliance on open-source data and the use of standard training techniques, prevent it from achieving a higher score, but the framework's potential for widespread adoption and its impact on the field are significant.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### GRAIL:Learning to Interact with Large Knowledge Graphs for Retrieval Augmented Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05498v1)
- **Authors**: Ge Chang, Jinbo Su, Jiacheng Liu, Pengfei Yang, Yuhao Shang, Huiwen Zheng, Hongli Ma, Yan Liang, Yuanchun Li, Yunxin Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) integrated with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) techniques have exhibited remarkable performance across a wide range of domains. However, existing RAG approaches primarily operate on unstructured data and demonstrate limited capability in handling structured knowledge such as knowledge graphs. Meanwhile, current graph retrieval methods fundamentally struggle to capture holistic graph structures while simultaneously facing precision control challenges that manifest as either critical information gaps or excessive redundant connections, collectively undermining reasoning performance. To address this challenge, we propose GRAIL: Graph-Retrieval Augmented Interactive Learning, a framework designed to interact with large-scale graphs for retrieval-augmented reasoning. Specifically, GRAIL integrates LLM-guided random exploration with path filtering to establish a data synthesis pipeline, where a fine-grained reasoning trajectory is automatically generated for each task. Based on the synthesized data, we then employ a two-stage training process to learn a policy that dynamically decides the optimal actions at each reasoning step. The overall objective of precision-conciseness balance in graph retrieval is decoupled into fine-grained process-supervised rewards to enhance data efficiency and training stability. In practical deployment, GRAIL adopts an interactive retrieval paradigm, enabling the model to autonomously explore graph paths while dynamically balancing retrieval breadth and precision. Extensive experiments have shown that GRAIL achieves an average accuracy improvement of 21.01% and F1 improvement of 22.43% on three knowledge graph question-answering datasets. Our source code and datasets is available at https://github.com/Changgeww/GRAIL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GRAIL paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRAIL (Graph-Retrieval Augmented Interactive Learning), a framework for enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks by enabling them to interact with large-scale knowledge graphs (KGs).  GRAIL addresses the limitations of existing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approaches, which primarily focus on unstructured data and struggle to effectively leverage structured knowledge within KGs. GRAIL employs a reinforcement learning (RL) agent that autonomously navigates KGs, dynamically exploring nodes and edges to retrieve relevant information. The framework features a data synthesis pipeline driven by LLMs, combined with a two-stage training process: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for basic alignment and RL for reasoning and exploration.  Key features include interactive retrieval, a data processing stage that filters trajectories, and process-level rewards for RL training. Experimental results on WebQSP, CWQ, and MetaQA demonstrate significant improvements over existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a well-integrated system that cleverly combines several existing techniques to address a practical problem.

    *   *Strengths:* The concept of an interactive agent exploring a KG is intuitive and aligns with how humans approach problem-solving. The use of LLMs for data synthesis and the two-stage training approach are also well-considered.
    *   *Weaknesses:* Many individual components are not entirely novel. The approach relies heavily on the quality of the LLM used for data synthesis, and the gains are potentially limited by the inherent capabilities of these models. The RL component is reliant on an LLM-grounded reward function, which is itself a complex undertaking.

*   **Significance:** Improving the ability of LLMs to reason with structured knowledge is a valuable goal with broad applications.

    *   *Strengths:* The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing approaches on standard KG question answering benchmarks. The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of individual components.
    *   *Weaknesses:* The dependence on proprietary LLMs (GPT-4, Qwen3) raises concerns about reproducibility and accessibility.  The framework seems complex to implement. There is a concern that the experiments only cover KGQA benchmarks, which are well-studied but perhaps not representative of the broader use cases of RAG. The impact on real-world tasks remains to be demonstrated.

*   **Rigor:** The paper is well-written, and the methodology is clearly described. The experiments are comprehensive, and the analysis is thorough.

    *   *Strengths:* The ablation studies are helpful in understanding the contribution of each component. The evaluation metrics are standard for the task.
    *   *Weaknesses:* A more detailed discussion of limitations, particularly concerning the data synthesis process and the reliance on strong LLMs, would enhance the paper's credibility. It could be worth exploring alternative KG architectures or benchmarking against other competitive KG reasoning methods.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow. The diagrams and tables are helpful in understanding the methodology and results.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

GRAIL offers a practical and effective approach to enhance LLMs' ability to interact with knowledge graphs.  While its individual components are not entirely groundbreaking, the integration of LLM-guided exploration, a two-stage training process, and interactive retrieval is well-executed. The performance gains on KGQA benchmarks are notable. The main concern is the strong dependence on proprietary LLMs, and the complexity of the method may limit wider adoption.
