- **Score**: 6/10

### Iterative Learning of Computable Phenotypes for Treatment Resistant Hypertension using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05581v1)
- **Authors**: Guilherme Seidyo Imai Aldeia, Daniel S. Herman, William G. La Cava
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities for medical question answering and programming, but their potential for generating interpretable computable phenotypes (CPs) is under-explored. In this work, we investigate whether LLMs can generate accurate and concise CPs for six clinical phenotypes of varying complexity, which could be leveraged to enable scalable clinical decision support to improve care for patients with hypertension. In addition to evaluating zero-short performance, we propose and test a synthesize, execute, debug, instruct strategy that uses LLMs to generate and iteratively refine CPs using data-driven feedback. Our results show that LLMs, coupled with iterative learning, can generate interpretable and reasonably accurate programs that approach the performance of state-of-the-art ML methods while requiring significantly fewer training examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate interpretable computable phenotypes (CPs) for treatment-resistant hypertension (TRH) and related conditions.  It proposes an iterative learning strategy involving "synthesize, execute, debug, instruct" (SEDI) to refine CPs using data-driven feedback.  The LLMs were prompted with varying levels of detail regarding the phenotype definition and feature sets.  The generated CPs were then compared to those produced by traditional interpretable machine learning (ML) methods like decision trees, logistic regression, and symbolic regression. The results suggest LLMs can generate reasonably accurate and concise CPs, and that iterative refinement via SEDI improves their performance.  In some cases, the LLM-generated CPs, particularly those refined through SEDI and parameter optimization, approached or exceeded the performance of state-of-the-art ML methods, requiring significantly fewer training examples and offering improved interpretability. The code, data, and methods are made public.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using LLMs for *CP generation*, rather than solely for prediction or question-answering in healthcare, is a strong point of novelty. While LLMs have been applied to NLP tasks in healthcare, and for drafting SQL queries representing phenotypes, the combination of LLMs, SEDI, and focus on *interpretable* and *executable* CPs is unique. Prior efforts mainly focus on zero-shot applications, while this paper explores iterative improvement. Furthermore, the comparison to established interpretable ML techniques is valuable.
*   **Significance:**  The potential for LLMs to automate the creation and refinement of CPs is highly significant, particularly in areas like hypertension management, where there are opportunities for improved screening and clinical decision support. The scalability aspect of automated CP generation (adaptation across localities/cohorts and over time) is a major advantage over manual CP curation. The improvement in performance through fewer curated samples is also a significant finding, potentially addressing a major challenge in healthcare ML.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined research questions.
    *   Rigorous experimental design, including comparisons to established ML methods.
    *   The SEDI framework is a compelling and practical approach for iterative refinement.
    *   Demonstration of competitive or superior performance relative to ML methods with less data.
    *   Explicit focus on interpretability (though indirectly measured via model complexity).
    *   Attention to potential bias in generated models, including gender and race.
    *   The paper provides specific code.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Interpretability is approximated by size, which is debatable; closer examination of code by experts would be a value.
    *   The generalizability of the results beyond hypertension is not explicitly explored.  While the methods are presented generally, the feature sets and specific iterative refinements may be highly domain-specific.
    *   The LLMs don't inherently use causal reasoning, so spurious correlations are likely in CPs.
    *   The potential biases and safety concerns associated with LLMs are acknowledged but not addressed in depth.
    *   Lack of comparison to additional LLMs such as gpt-01, Claude or llama.
    *   It's not perfectly clear how robust their bias evaluation is. For example, Black men have "best" discriminative performance. Why should it be "worst" performing women that are discussed? It could simply be a statistical variation.
    *   While the SEDI significantly reduces the number of expert curated samples, is it truly "automated review and refinement", or merely automated refinement?

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work could stimulate further research into using LLMs for CP generation across various clinical domains.  The SEDI strategy may become a common approach for refining LLM-based models in healthcare applications where interpretability and data efficiency are crucial. It also encourages a shift toward explicit CP generation rather than solely relying on black-box predictive models. The open-source release of their SEDI framework and code also encourages other researchers to readily explore the development of CPs for other conditions.
*   **Significance for Healthcare:** LLMs are a speculative area in healthcare, but in an intermediate step, they could be used to create final computable phenotypes for using in clinical practice.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel approach with promising results, demonstrating that LLMs, when coupled with iterative refinement techniques, can generate interpretable and reasonably accurate models for computable phenotyping.  The SEDI framework and the comparison to established ML methods provide a solid foundation. However, the limitations regarding generalizability, bias, and the somewhat indirect measure of interpretability prevent a higher score. Also, their bias section is open to misinterpretation. It represents a significant contribution to the field but requires further work to address the limitations and expand its applicability.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MathSmith: Towards Extremely Hard Mathematical Reasoning by Forging Synthetic Problems with a Reinforced Policy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05592v1)
- **Authors**: Shaoxiong Zhan, Yanlin Lai, Ziyu Lu, Dahua Lin, Ziqing Yang, Fei Tang
- **Abstract**: Large language models have achieved substantial progress in mathematical reasoning, yet their advancement is limited by the scarcity of high-quality, high-difficulty training data. Existing synthesis methods largely rely on transforming human-written templates, limiting both diversity and scalability. We propose MathSmith, a novel framework for synthesizing challenging mathematical problems to enhance LLM reasoning. Rather than modifying existing problems, MathSmith constructs new ones from scratch by randomly sampling concept-explanation pairs from PlanetMath, ensuring data independence and avoiding contamination. To increase difficulty, we design nine predefined strategies as soft constraints during rationales. We further adopts reinforcement learning to jointly optimize structural validity, reasoning complexity, and answer consistency. The length of the reasoning trace generated under autoregressive prompting is used to reflect cognitive complexity, encouraging the creation of more demanding problems aligned with long-chain-of-thought reasoning. Experiments across five benchmarks, categorized as easy & medium (GSM8K, MATH-500) and hard (AIME2024, AIME2025, OlympiadBench), show that MathSmith consistently outperforms existing baselines under both short and long CoT settings. Additionally, a weakness-focused variant generation module enables targeted improvement on specific concepts. Overall, MathSmith exhibits strong scalability, generalization, and transferability, highlighting the promise of high-difficulty synthetic data in advancing LLM reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MathSmith paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MathSmith, a novel framework designed to generate challenging mathematical problems for training large language models (LLMs).  Unlike existing methods that modify human-written templates, MathSmith creates problems from scratch by randomly sampling concept-explanation pairs from PlanetMath, ensuring data independence and reducing contamination. The framework uses nine predefined difficulty strategies as soft constraints during rationale generation and employs reinforcement learning (RL) to jointly optimize structural validity, reasoning complexity (estimated by CoT length), and answer consistency. A weakness-focused variant generation module allows targeted improvement on specific concepts. Experiments on various benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH-500, AIME2024, AIME2025, OlympiadBench) show MathSmith outperforms baselines, especially under long CoT settings, demonstrating strong scalability, generalization, and transferability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty in Problem Generation:** The primary strength lies in its approach to problem generation. Moving away from template-based methods and generating problems from fundamental mathematical concepts is a significant step towards autonomous problem creation. This addresses the limitations of existing methods in diversity and scalability. The random sampling of concept-explanation pairs is an effective way to avoid contamination, a prevalent issue with finetuning on existing datasets.
*   **Reinforcement Learning for Difficulty Control:** Using RL to optimize for structural validity, complexity, and answer consistency is a clever technique. In particular, using the length of the reasoning trace as a proxy for cognitive complexity is an interesting and potentially valuable heuristic. It aligns well with the observed relationship between problem difficulty and CoT length.
*   **Weakness-Focused Adaptation:** The weakness-focused generation module is a crucial element, allowing for targeted improvement on specific concepts where the model struggles. This iterative refinement loop enhances the framework's practical utility.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results consistently demonstrate MathSmith's superiority across various benchmarks and settings, including both short and long CoT prompting. The relative improvements on hard benchmarks are particularly noteworthy. The scaling experiments further solidify the claims of scalability and effectiveness.
*   **Focus on Reasoning Depth:**  The paper addresses the scarcity of training data to enhance reasoning for LLMs. It takes the innovative approach to create synthetic problems, inducing LLMs with longer reasoning sequences. This adds depth to LLMs' knowledge and is worth exploring.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-40 for Initial Generation:** The initial cold-start data generation depends on GPT-40. While it's used to create the initial dataset, it introduces a potential bias and limitation. The quality of the initial samples directly impacts the subsequent fine-tuning and RL stages. The model might only reach a certain ceiling of improvement.

*   **CoT Length as a Perfect Proxy:**  While CoT length correlates with difficulty, it's not a perfect metric. A model might generate verbose, irrelevant reasoning steps that artificially inflate the CoT length without reflecting genuine complexity. There can be some noise in the signal.
*   **Computational Cost:** The reliance on RL, particularly with large models and teacher inference during reward calculation, introduces substantial computational demands. This limits the accessibility and scalability of the method, especially for researchers with limited resources.
*   **Over-Emphasis on Olympiad Style:** While the emphasis on high-difficulty problems is understandable, it might create a bias toward problems that resemble Olympiad-level questions. This could potentially limit the model's generalization to other mathematical problem types.

*   **Some Results Show Weakness in Easy Mode:** The trend can be seen with GSMBK. This probably stems from the word problem dataset nature. While the method emphasizes synthesis and high-difficulty problems, this can hinder performance.
*   **Need for Detailed Architectural Insights:** Future work on how to improve LLM capacity with better training and data generation is needed.

**Significance:**

MathSmith represents a significant advancement in the field of mathematical problem generation. Its autonomous approach and effective difficulty control mechanisms address key limitations of existing methods. The framework has the potential to substantially improve the reasoning capabilities of LLMs by providing them with more diverse and challenging training data. The emphasis on verifiable synthetic problems allows future models to improve on logical consistency.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

MathSmith offers a novel and significant approach to mathematical problem generation, addressing critical limitations in existing methodologies and demonstrating impressive empirical results.  The RL-based difficulty control and weakness-focused refinement are particularly valuable contributions. While there are concerns regarding GPT-40 reliance, CoT length as a perfect proxy, and computational cost, the overall impact of the paper is high. It paves the way for more autonomous and scalable methods for generating challenging mathematical datasets, which is crucial for advancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The innovative approach to source concept data from PlanetMath and focus on multi-objective rewards to better guide the language models' capabilities further solidifies the strong contributions to synthetic data generation and LLM training. Thus, an '8' reflects its notable novelty, significant improvements over existing approaches, and clear potential to influence the future direction of research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLaVA-RE: Binary Image-Text Relevancy Evaluation with Multimodal Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05602v1)
- **Authors**: Tao Sun, Oliver Liu, JinJin Li, Lan Ma
- **Abstract**: Multimodal generative AI usually involves generating image or text responses given inputs in another modality. The evaluation of image-text relevancy is essential for measuring response quality or ranking candidate responses. In particular, binary relevancy evaluation, i.e., ``Relevant'' vs. ``Not Relevant'', is a fundamental problem. However, this is a challenging task considering that texts have diverse formats and the definition of relevancy varies in different scenarios. We find that Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are an ideal choice to build such evaluators, as they can flexibly handle complex text formats and take in additional task information. In this paper, we present LLaVA-RE, a first attempt for binary image-text relevancy evaluation with MLLM. It follows the LLaVA architecture and adopts detailed task instructions and multimodal in-context samples. In addition, we propose a novel binary relevancy data set that covers various tasks. Experimental results validate the effectiveness of our framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LLaVA-RE: Binary Image-Text Relevancy Evaluation with Multimodal Large Language Model" introduces a method for evaluating the relevancy between images and complex text using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It builds upon the LLaVA architecture, incorporating detailed task instructions and multimodal in-context learning to improve performance. The authors create a new binary relevancy dataset covering diverse tasks, addressing the lack of publicly available datasets in this area. They train and evaluate their model, LLaVA-RE, demonstrating its effectiveness compared to the vanilla LLaVA model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on binary image-text relevancy *evaluation*, specifically using MLLMs. While MLLMs have been used for various VQA tasks, the application to relevancy with complex and diverse text formats is a valuable contribution. Creating a dedicated dataset for this purpose is also significant.  However, the core architecture is LLaVA, and the modifications are primarily around task instruction and data creation.  The in-context learning approach is not radically different from other work in the area.

*   **Significance:** Accurate image-text relevancy evaluation is crucial for many AI applications, including AI assistants, search engines, and content generation. The paper addresses a real-world problem where existing similarity-based methods fall short due to long and ambiguous texts. By utilizing the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs and introducing the concept of fine-grained relevant data, the work potentially advances the state-of-the-art in this area. The release of the created dataset would also benefit the research community, but this depends on the approval of the dataset release.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a practical and important problem.
    *   It leverages the strengths of MLLMs for complex reasoning and text understanding.
    *   The introduction of a novel dataset fills a gap in available resources.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core model is built upon an existing architecture (LLaVA 1.5). Thus, the novelty might be considered incremental.
    *   The paper relies heavily on Claude 3 Sonnet for generating instructions and demonstration examples. While using such tools is practical, the reliance on a closed-source model could hinder reproducibility and open research.
    *   The limited shot experiments do not present significant improvements; the paper attributes this to ICL and fine-grained tasks.

*   **Potential Impact:** The proposed framework and dataset have the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable image-text relevancy evaluation methods. Improved evaluation metrics could lead to better performance in various downstream applications.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by applying MLLMs to the relatively under-explored area of binary image-text relevancy evaluation, particularly when dealing with complex text formats. The creation of a dedicated dataset is also a significant asset. However, the incremental nature of the architectural changes (building on LLaVA 1.5) and the reliance on a proprietary LLM for data generation temper the overall impact. Also, there is a potential release issue for the generated dataset, which may limit its future use. While in-context learning helps performance, the limited shot experiments show a relatively small improvement on the proposed fine-grained datasets.

### Uni-cot: Towards Unified Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Text and Vision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05606v1)
- **Authors**: Luozheng Qin, Jia Gong, Yuqing Sun, Tianjiao Li, Mengping Yang, Xiaomeng Yang, Chao Qu, Zhiyu Tan, Hao Li
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been widely adopted to enhance Large Language Models (LLMs) by decomposing complex tasks into simpler, sequential subtasks. However, extending CoT to vision-language reasoning tasks remains challenging, as it often requires interpreting transitions of visual states to support reasoning. Existing methods often struggle with this due to limited capacity of modeling visual state transitions or incoherent visual trajectories caused by fragmented architectures. To overcome these limitations, we propose Uni-CoT, a Unified Chain-of-Thought framework that enables coherent and grounded multimodal reasoning within a single unified model. The key idea is to leverage a model capable of both image understanding and generation to reason over visual content and model evolving visual states. However, empowering a unified model to achieve that is non-trivial, given the high computational cost and the burden of training. To address this, Uni-CoT introduces a novel two-level reasoning paradigm: A Macro-Level CoT for high-level task planning and A Micro-Level CoT for subtask execution. This design significantly reduces the computational overhead. Furthermore, we introduce a structured training paradigm that combines interleaved image-text supervision for macro-level CoT with multi-task objectives for micro-level CoT. Together, these innovations allow Uni-CoT to perform scalable and coherent multi-modal reasoning. Furthermore, thanks to our design, all experiments can be efficiently completed using only 8 A100 GPUs with 80GB VRAM each. Experimental results on reasoning-driven image generation benchmark (WISE) and editing benchmarks (RISE and KRIS) indicates that Uni-CoT demonstrates SOTA performance and strong generalization, establishing Uni-CoT as a promising solution for multi-modal reasoning. Project Page and Code: https://sais-fuxi.github.io/projects/uni-cot/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Uni-CoT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Uni-CoT, a novel framework for multi-modal (text and vision) reasoning.  It extends the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) approach, commonly used to enhance the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs), to handle tasks requiring joint reasoning over text and images.  The core idea is to use a unified model, BAGEL, capable of both image understanding and generation, to model visual state transitions in a coherent manner.  To address the computational complexity inherent in multi-modal CoT, the framework employs a two-level reasoning paradigm: a Macro-Level CoT for high-level task planning and a Micro-Level CoT for detailed subtask execution.  The authors also introduce a structured training paradigm that combines interleaved image-text supervision with multi-task auxiliary objectives. Experimental results on image generation and editing benchmarks (WISE, RISE, KRIS) demonstrate state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* and *structured application* of existing ideas within a single framework, rather than fundamentally new algorithms.
    *   The use of CoT for reasoning is well-established.
    *   Unified multi-modal models exist (BAGEL is leveraged, not invented).
    *   Hierarchical reasoning has been explored previously.
    *   Masked attention is an established technique.

    However, the specific combination of these elements—the two-level CoT with a unified image understanding and generation model, alongside a structured training paradigm with masked attention and the MDP-based subtask execution—is indeed a novel contribution. The two-level hierarchical design with Macro and Micro CoT helps to resolve the computational complexity.

* **Significance:**  The potential impact is substantial for several reasons:

    *   The ability to perform coherent and grounded multi-modal reasoning is a crucial step towards more capable and versatile AI systems.  Bridging the gap between vision and language understanding is a key challenge.
    *   The framework's efficiency (trained on a relatively small number of GPUs) makes it accessible to a wider range of researchers and practitioners.
    *   The strong performance on benchmarks suggests the framework is practical and effective.

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing approaches to multi-modal reasoning.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:**  The Uni-CoT framework is well-structured and clearly explained.  The descriptions of the Macro-Level and Micro-Level CoTs are detailed.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The empirical evaluation on relevant benchmarks demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach. The experiments compare against strong baselines, including Gemini.
    *   **Detailed Training and Evaluation Details:**  The paper provides enough detail about the experimental setup, data curation, and training procedure to enable reproducibility.
    *   **Addressing Computational Complexity:** The hierarchical reasoning structure is a smart design to address computational complexity.

* **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on BAGEL:** The framework is tightly coupled with the BAGEL model.  While leveraging an existing model is reasonable, it limits the generality and potential for adoption if BAGEL has its own limitations or falls out of favor. This dependency should be clearly acknowledged.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Planning Strategies:**  The Macro-Level CoT planning strategies are presented, but the empirical evaluation might benefit from a more in-depth comparison of their relative effectiveness. The paper mentions that more advanced strategies are difficult to achieve stable training, but doesn't explain why this is the case in sufficient detail.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** While there's a qualitative analysis section, it's a bit cursory. Showing more examples of the reasoning process, especially cases where Uni-CoT succeeds while other methods fail, would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Need More Details on Visual Preference Learning:**  The reinforcement learning aspect, particularly the "Visual Preference Learning," is not fully detailed.  A future version promises more information, but its absence weakens the current presentation.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies**: The paper could benefit from more thorough ablation studies to dissect the contribution of each component in Uni-CoT.

* **Justification for Score:**

Uni-CoT presents a valuable advancement in multi-modal reasoning. The combination of CoT, hierarchical reasoning, and unified models, coupled with effective training techniques, yields a practical and performant framework.  While the *individual components* aren't entirely new, the *integrated system* and the demonstrated results on challenging benchmarks justify a relatively high score. However, its dependency on the BAGEL model, limitations in planning strategies, missing details on preference learning and the superficiality of qualitative analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. More extensive studies would be valuable to the community.

### Shuffle-R1: Efficient RL framework for Multimodal Large Language Models via Data-centric Dynamic Shuffle
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05612v1)
- **Authors**: Linghao Zhu, Yiran Guan, Dingkang Liang, Jianzhong Ju, Zhenbo Luo, Bin Qin, Jian Luan, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an effective post-training paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language model (MLLM). However, current RL pipelines often suffer from training inefficiencies caused by two underexplored issues: Advantage Collapsing, where most advantages in a batch concentrate near zero, and Rollout Silencing, where the proportion of rollouts contributing non-zero gradients diminishes over time. These issues lead to suboptimal gradient updates and hinder long-term learning efficiency. To address these issues, we propose Shuffle-R1, a simple yet principled framework that improves RL fine-tuning efficiency by dynamically restructuring trajectory sampling and batch composition. It introduces (1) Pairwise Trajectory Sampling, which selects high-contrast trajectories with large advantages to improve gradient signal quality, and (2) Advantage-based Trajectory Shuffle, which increases exposure of valuable rollouts through informed batch reshuffling. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks show that our framework consistently outperforms strong RL baselines with minimal overhead. These results highlight the importance of data-centric adaptations for more efficient RL training in MLLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Shuffle-R1: Efficient RL framework for Multimodal Large Language Models via Data-centric Dynamic Shuffle":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Shuffle-R1, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the efficiency of fine-tuning multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The core idea is to address two observed inefficiencies in standard RL training: Advantage Collapsing (where most advantages concentrate near zero) and Rollout Silencing (where the proportion of rollouts contributing non-zero gradients decreases over time). Shuffle-R1 tackles these issues with two key modules:
1.  **Pairwise Trajectory Sampling (PTS):**  Selects high-contrast trajectory pairs with large-magnitude advantages to improve gradient signal quality.
2.  **Advantage-based Batch Shuffle (ABS):** Dynamically reshapes training batches to prioritize informative trajectories while down-weighting ineffective ones, alleviating rollout silencing.
The authors demonstrate through experiments on various reasoning benchmarks that Shuffle-R1 outperforms strong RL baselines with minimal computational overhead, highlighting the importance of data-centric adaptations for efficient RL training in MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its data-centric approach to improving RL training efficiency. While existing works often focus on reward engineering or objective function optimization, Shuffle-R1 directly addresses data sampling and batch construction, uncovering and mitigating Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing. The pairwise sampling and batch shuffling strategies are simple to implement yet effective. The identification and articulation of the Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing phenomena are themselves noteworthy contributions.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the potential to make RL fine-tuning of MLLMs more efficient and accessible. By addressing training inefficiencies, Shuffle-R1 could reduce the computational resources required to achieve state-of-the-art performance on multimodal reasoning tasks. This is particularly important as MLLMs become increasingly complex and resource-intensive to train.  The performance on challenging benchmarks, and comparison to closed-source models such as GPT-4o and Claude-3.7 further increases the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the issues of Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing, providing empirical evidence for their existence.
    *   **Principled Approach:** Shuffle-R1's modules are grounded in the understanding of gradient update dynamics.  The designs of PTS and ABS follow logically from the problems they address.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple reasoning benchmarks, model scales, and comparisons to strong baselines.  Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each module.
    *   **Simplicity and Integrability:**  The proposed framework is lightweight and can be easily integrated with existing RL algorithms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of Shuffle-R1 may be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters such as the sampling ratio and shuffle times. While the authors conduct an ablation study on these parameters, further investigation into robust settings across diverse tasks would be beneficial.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides a strong empirical analysis, a more formal theoretical justification for the effectiveness of Shuffle-R1 would strengthen the claims. (e.g., showing how PTS/ABS reduces the variance of gradient estimates).
    *   **Limited Datasets:** All the experiments were based on open-source datasets on a single domain (geometry), limiting the generalization claims. A few experiments with other domains would increase the significance of the results.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Shuffle-R1 is likely to influence future research on efficient RL training for MLLMs.  The data-centric approach may inspire other researchers to explore novel ways of improving data sampling and batch construction.  The paper will likely be well-cited, and the techniques may be incorporated into standard RL training pipelines for MLLMs.

*   **Critical Justification for Score:**  While the approach is novel and the empirical results convincing, there is still the need for more theoretical justification and testing on a wider variety of datasets.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Cooper: Co-Optimizing Policy and Reward Models in Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05613v1)
- **Authors**: Haitao Hong, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Guiyang Hou, Wenqi Zhang, Weiming Lu, Yongliang Shen, Jun Xiao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in reasoning tasks, where reinforcement learning (RL) serves as a key algorithm for enhancing their reasoning capabilities. Currently, there are two mainstream reward paradigms: model-based rewards and rule-based rewards. However, both approaches suffer from limitations: rule-based rewards lack robustness, while model-based rewards are vulnerable to reward hacking. To address these issues, we propose Cooper(Co-optimizing Policy Model and Reward Model), a RL framework that jointly optimizes both the policy model and the reward model. Cooper leverages the high precision of rule-based rewards when identifying correct responses, and dynamically constructs and selects positive-negative sample pairs for continued training the reward model. This design enhances robustness and mitigates the risk of reward hacking. To further support Cooper, we introduce a hybrid annotation strategy that efficiently and accurately generates training data for the reward model. We also propose a reference-based reward modeling paradigm, where the reward model takes a reference answer as input. Based on this design, we train a reward model named VerifyRM, which achieves higher accuracy on VerifyBench compared to other models of the same size. We conduct reinforcement learning using both VerifyRM and Cooper. Our experiments show that Cooper not only alleviates reward hacking but also improves end-to-end RL performance, for instance, achieving a 0.54% gain in average accuracy on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct. Our findings demonstrate that dynamically updating reward model is an effective way to combat reward hacking, providing a reference for better integrating reward models into RL.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "COOPER: CO-OPTIMIZING POLICY AND REWARD MODELS IN REINFORCEMENT LEARNING FOR LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS."

**Summary:**

This paper introduces Cooper, a novel Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework designed for training Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance their reasoning abilities. Cooper addresses the limitations of existing reward paradigms, particularly the lack of robustness in rule-based rewards and the vulnerability to reward hacking in model-based rewards. The core idea is to co-optimize both the policy model and the reward model during RL training. Cooper leverages the precision of rule-based rewards to identify correct responses and then uses this information to dynamically construct positive/negative sample pairs to refine the reward model. This dynamic update is intended to improve robustness and resist reward hacking. The paper also introduces a hybrid annotation strategy and a reference-based reward modeling paradigm (VerifyRM) for improved reward model accuracy. Experimental results suggest that Cooper alleviates reward hacking and improves end-to-end RL performance, particularly on challenging mathematical reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of co-optimizing the policy and reward models is a valuable contribution, especially in the context of LLMs. Dynamic update of the reward model is a direct response to the common issue of reward hacking. The hybrid annotation strategy to generate training data and reference-based reward modelling paradigm (VerifyRM) are useful additions that aim to resolve existing problems regarding data creation and RM accuracy in RLHF for LLMs. While there's existing research on RL for LLMs and reward model training, the specific combination of these elements into a unified framework with the goal of reducing reward hacking and increasing robustness seems genuinely novel.

*   **Significance:** Reward hacking is a well-known problem in RL, and this paper provides a concrete solution and potentially opens up a pathway for more robust RL training. The reported improvements in performance, especially on difficult reasoning tasks, suggest practical value. If the approach is generalizable to other tasks and LLMs, it could significantly impact how RL is used for LLM training. In addition, the new dataset and reward model will provide useful benchmarks for others in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-defined solution:** Cooper is a coherent and well-explained framework.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experiments provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of Cooper.
    *   **Novel techniques:** A well-designed hybrid annotation and reference-based reward modelling paradigm (VerifyRM).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited generalizability:** The experiments are focused on mathematical reasoning. It's unclear how well Cooper will transfer to other types of reasoning tasks or other application domains.
    *   **Complexity:** Co-optimizing the policy and reward models adds complexity to the training process, which might make it harder to implement and scale.
    *   **Reliance on Rule-Based Reward Precision:** Although addressed by selecting correct samples, the algorithm still hinges on the initial precision of the rule-based system. In problems where generating such systems is complex, results might be limited.
    *   **Assistant LLM for negative sampling:** Reliance on assistant LLM for negative sampling introduces another potential source of error.

*   **Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RL for LLMs by highlighting the importance of dynamic reward models and by providing a practical framework for co-optimization. The verifyRM is a useful addition and should be of interest to researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the identified strengths and weaknesses, I would rate this paper as a **7**. It presents a novel framework that tackles a significant problem (reward hacking) in RL for LLMs, validated by promising experimental results and offers specific techniques (hybrid annotation, reference reward modeling) that are immediately useful to the field.
The key limitations are the lack of generalizability and potential increase in complexity. It is likely that Cooper's principles can be applied to other reasoning or problem-solving tasks that have high-precision rule-based reward signals available. The framework does rely on certain components that may become sources of additional problems. Even so, the strengths outweigh these limitations, making it a valuable and significant contribution to the field.

### OmniEAR: Benchmarking Agent Reasoning in Embodied Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05614v1)
- **Authors**: Zixuan Wang, Dingming Li, Hongxing Li, Shuo Chen, Yuchen Yan, Wenqi Zhang, Yongliang Shen, Weiming Lu, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang
- **Abstract**: Large language models excel at abstract reasoning but their capacity for embodied agent reasoning remains largely unexplored. We present OmniEAR, a comprehensive framework for evaluating how language models reason about physical interactions, tool usage, and multi-agent coordination in embodied tasks. Unlike existing benchmarks that provide predefined tool sets or explicit collaboration directives, OmniEAR requires agents to dynamically acquire capabilities and autonomously determine coordination strategies based on task demands. Through text-based environment representation, we model continuous physical properties and complex spatial relationships across 1,500 scenarios spanning household and industrial domains. Our systematic evaluation reveals severe performance degradation when models must reason from constraints: while achieving 85-96% success with explicit instructions, performance drops to 56-85% for tool reasoning and 63-85% for implicit collaboration, with compound tasks showing over 50% failure rates. Surprisingly, complete environmental information degrades coordination performance, indicating models cannot filter task-relevant constraints. Fine-tuning improves single-agent tasks dramatically (0.6% to 76.3%) but yields minimal multi-agent gains (1.5% to 5.5%), exposing fundamental architectural limitations. These findings demonstrate that embodied reasoning poses fundamentally different challenges than current models can address, establishing OmniEAR as a rigorous benchmark for evaluating and advancing embodied AI systems. Our code and data are included in the supplementary materials and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniEAR, a new benchmark for evaluating the embodied reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing benchmarks, OmniEAR emphasizes reasoning from physical constraints, dynamic capability acquisition through tool use, and autonomous multi-agent coordination.  The framework includes EAR-Sim, a simulator that uses text-based representations to model continuous physical properties and spatial relationships, and EAR-Bench, a suite of 1,500 scenarios spanning household and industrial domains.  The authors evaluate several LLMs, finding that performance degrades significantly when models are required to reason from physical constraints, acquire capabilities, or autonomously coordinate, despite performing well on explicit instructions. They also find that model scale does not always translate to improved embodied reasoning and that complete environmental information can sometimes hinder performance. The study includes a fine-tuning experiment, but the benefit mostly remains in single-agent tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its holistic approach to embodied reasoning. While existing benchmarks might focus on individual aspects like tool use or multi-agent collaboration, OmniEAR integrates these elements and, crucially, emphasizes reasoning from implicit physical constraints. The use of text-based representations for the environment and objects, while not entirely new in itself, is strategically employed to enable large-scale evaluation and to highlight the challenges of grounding language in a physically plausible context. The focus on generating scenarios where collaboration naturally emerges from limitations, as opposed to explicit instructions, is a significant step forward. The dynamic capability acquisition via tools is also a strong point.
*   **Significance:** The findings of the paper have the potential to be significant. The demonstration that current LLMs struggle with embodied reasoning tasks that require understanding physical constraints and autonomous coordination exposes a critical gap in the capabilities of these models. The observation that simply increasing model scale does not automatically solve these problems is also important. This implies that architectural innovations and training strategies specifically designed to address embodied reasoning are needed. By pinpointing these limitations, OmniEAR provides a valuable roadmap for future research. The provided data sets for analysis are of very high quality.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** OmniEAR is a well-designed benchmark that addresses multiple aspects of embodied reasoning.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation of several LLMs and provide detailed analysis of the results.
    *   **Identified Limitations:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current LLMs in embodied reasoning.
    *   **High-Quality Datasets**: The data set and the simulator are carefully created and meticulously curated and cover important aspects of simulation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Text-Based Abstraction:** While the text-based environment representation is a strength for scalability, it also abstracts away some aspects of real-world embodied interaction, such as continuous control and sensorimotor feedback. This limitation is acknowledged by the authors, but it's important to recognize that the benchmark may not fully capture the complexities of physical interaction.
    *   **Limited Fine-Tuning Results:** The fine-tuning experiment did not produce strong benefits in multi-agent tasks, suggesting a need for more sophisticated training strategies or architectural changes.
    *   **Limited Model Scope**: The evaluations in the work might be limited due to the fact that it contains LLMs only, not the approaches specialized for the embodied tasks.
*   **Potential Influence:** OmniEAR has the potential to influence the development of new LLM architectures and training strategies specifically designed for embodied reasoning. It could also lead to a better understanding of how to ground language in physical contexts and how to enable autonomous coordination in multi-agent systems.
*   **Rigorous Justification of score:** The OmniEAR benchmark addresses an important challenge in the intersection of LLMs and robotics: embodied reasoning. The paper demonstrates that existing LLMs struggle with certain crucial reasoning aspects. The key contributions, focus on implicit physical reasoning as opposed to explicit instruction following and dynamic capabilities, distinguish it from current benchmarks, highlighting critical shortcomings in current AI systems. Furthermore, the paper offers a good analysis of those findings, albeit with some limitations.
*   The evaluation contains just LLMs. There could be additional analysis if there are robotics-specialized methods in comparison.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** OmniEAR is a valuable contribution to the field of embodied AI. Its emphasis on reasoning from physical constraints, dynamic capability acquisition, and autonomous coordination addresses a critical gap in the capabilities of current LLMs. The paper's well-designed benchmark, systematic evaluation, and clear identification of limitations make it a significant step forward. However, some limitations, mainly the text-based nature, are present. While the novelty is adequate, the potential impact on the field, particularly in guiding future research on embodied AI and the findings of current LLMs falling short make the benchmark significant and will potentially result in the paper being impactful.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TrajEvo: Trajectory Prediction Heuristics Design via LLM-driven Evolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05616v1)
- **Authors**: Zhikai Zhao, Chuanbo Hua, Federico Berto, Kanghoon Lee, Zihan Ma, Jiachen Li, Jinkyoo Park
- **Abstract**: Trajectory prediction is a critical task in modeling human behavior, especially in safety-critical domains such as social robotics and autonomous vehicle navigation. Traditional heuristics based on handcrafted rules often lack accuracy and generalizability. Although deep learning approaches offer improved performance, they typically suffer from high computational cost, limited explainability, and, importantly, poor generalization to out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. In this paper, we introduce TrajEvo, a framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically design trajectory prediction heuristics. TrajEvo employs an evolutionary algorithm to generate and refine prediction heuristics from past trajectory data. We propose two key innovations: Cross-Generation Elite Sampling to encourage population diversity, and a Statistics Feedback Loop that enables the LLM to analyze and improve alternative predictions. Our evaluations demonstrate that TrajEvo outperforms existing heuristic methods across multiple real-world datasets, and notably surpasses both heuristic and deep learning methods in generalizing to an unseen OOD real-world dataset. TrajEvo marks a promising step toward the automated design of fast, explainable, and generalizable trajectory prediction heuristics. We release our source code to facilitate future research at https://github.com/ai4co/trajevo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRAJEVO, a novel framework for automated design of trajectory prediction heuristics using Large Language Models (LLMs) and evolutionary algorithms (EAs). TRAJEVO leverages an evolutionary loop to generate, evaluate, and refine prediction heuristics from trajectory data. The framework incorporates two key innovations: Cross-Generation Elite Sampling (CGES) to encourage population diversity and a Statistics Feedback Loop (SFL) that allows the LLM to analyze heuristic performance and guide the generation of improved candidates. Experimental results demonstrate that TRAJEVO outperforms existing heuristic methods on several real-world datasets, even generalizing better than both heuristic and deep learning methods on an unseen out-of-distribution (OOD) dataset, while maintaining computational efficiency and interpretability. The paper emphasizes TRAJEVO's ability to create fast, explainable, and generalizable trajectory prediction heuristics compared to existing methods that may be accurate but lack explainability and have high computational costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel approach by integrating LLMs and evolutionary algorithms specifically for automated trajectory prediction heuristic design.  This differs from prior work primarily focusing on individual LLM or EA for generic algorithmic design, or those using deep learning to forecast trajectories. The CGES and SFL components also introduce innovations within the LLM-EA framework by improving the search process and refining the LLM's guidance.

**Significance:**

*   **Automated Heuristic Design:** Automating heuristic design addresses a significant gap, particularly where computational cost, explainability, and OOD generalization are important. The automatic generation of new high-performance heuristics is potentially very valuable, especially since manually crafting these rules is usually difficult and time-consuming.
*   **Computational Efficiency:**  The low computational cost of TRAJEVO-generated heuristics is a significant advantage, making them suitable for resource-constrained robots and vehicles where deep learning models may be infeasible. This real-time application is important.
*   **Explainability:** The generated Python code provides transparency and verifiability, critical for safety-critical applications. In contrast, deep learning black-box models lack this crucial advantage.
*   **Generalization:** The remarkable OOD generalization performance demonstrated on the SDD dataset is a strong selling point. The potential to operate safely in previously unseen scenarios is vital in robotics and autonomous systems.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **In-Distribution Performance Gap:** While the OOD performance is outstanding, TRAJEVO does not consistently surpass the most advanced deep learning models in-distribution (Table 2). This suggests a potential trade-off between generalization and specialized performance within a training distribution. The paper acknowledges this limitation, which is acceptable for a first iteration.
*   **Limited Input Modalities:** As the authors note, their current evaluations used only positional data. The potential to incorporate multi-modal sensory input remains unexplored, which could improve performance.
*   **Downstream Tasks:**  The system optimizes traditional trajectory prediction metrics. There remains a step between optimized trajectory predictions and their utility within the more complex task of planning robot motions (collision avoidance, navigation, etc.).

**Potential Influence:**

TRAJEVO has the potential to influence the following areas:

*   **Robotics and Autonomous Systems:** Provides a practical and reliable alternative to deep learning in resource-constrained systems.
*   **AI Algorithm Design:** Encourages further research into LLM-EA hybrid systems for other complex engineering problems.
*   **Trajectory Prediction Research:**  Shifts focus towards interpretable and generalizable solutions.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8/10**.  TRAJEVO represents a significant advancement in trajectory prediction by effectively bridging the gap between efficient heuristics and powerful but opaque deep learning methods. While some in-distribution performance remains to be gained, the OOD generalization and interpretability make this work important. The introduction of CGES and SFL as core components further enhance the novelty. If the issues of input modality and performance relative to SOTA solutions can be addressed in future, related work, then this could be a cornerstone in the field of robotics.
